CHAPTER 

YNN KENNEDY bent forward to her dressing table mirror

as she carefully outlined a larger and more generous mouth than her own really was. Even so, she did not conceal entirely a pouting, discontented curve that time had placed there, for no lipstick could have done that. She was smart and petite, with eyes that were almost too large for the rest of her face-the iris so dark that the whites looked blue by contrast. There was something smoldering in the depths of those brown eyes that gave Lynn's face its fascination. Her hair was dark and shiny and shoulder length. She pushed it back from her temples with her fingertips, touched each ear with a drop of her favorite perfume, a scent which would have been too spicy for most women but which suited her perfectly, and swung her compact little body around so that she faced out into the livingroom where her husband was puttering around with the assorted letters, advertisements, and bills that every livingroom desk accumulates. "Martin," she asked, "what time is it?" "Time you were ready," he answered, not unkindly. "They'll be here in a minute." "Well, I am! I'd always be dressed on time if I didn't have to stand over that hot stove getting sticky and messy every night. I 1

do wish we could at least afford a cook like everyone else. After I go through that muddle every night I'm almost too tired to bother. • · · 

Her voice was querulous and complaining.

Martin broke in, as if he had not been listening at all: "Lynn, what's this bill for?" "How would I know what it's for? What's this for? What's that for? You'll drive me completely crazy. Just what bill do you mean?"

She stood in the doorway, looking down at her husband. There was distaste in her eyes and a sign of danger in her voice, but Martin didn't look around. "It's from Tripler's," he answered quietly. "It's for a tie-five dollars and is marked 'Paid-taken."" "Well, Martin, if it's been paid, what in the world are you raising a row about? It's not mine. Probably someone dropped it on the floor and Gloria found it and put it in the desk. I do think that you might stay out of my drawer- I have absolutely no privacy."

She shrugged her shoulders in annoyance and sat down in a scarlet chair that flattered her dark coloring and outlined the soft curves of her body in her new gold jersey dress. 

Martin tossed the sales slip aside, gestured hopelessly, and walked over to the window. "All right," he suggested, "let's forget it. We're having guests tonight."

A cold gleam of exultation replaced the anger in Lynn's eyes. "Say, it's raining," Martin remarked, peering out of the window. "Well, I recall having seen rain before, Martin. I believe it's called a natural phenomenon and occurs fairly frequently." "It's so late-" "Now, where have I heard that word before? I believe, Martin, that 'late' and 'budget' are your two greatest satisfactions in life, if you have any." 2

"Lynn," Martin pleaded, "I thought we had agreed to drop it." "That's all very well for you, Martin. Naturally you'd like to drop it. You're always picking on me about something and then when I want to have it out, you say, 'Let's drop it.""

Martin walked over and put an arm around his wife. "All right, dearest," he appealed. "Let's not fight. It was all my fault for bringing up the subject." 

Lynn permitted a tear or two to roll down her pretty cheek, and choked an agreement: "Yes, it was, Martin. However, I'll forgive you."

Martin sighed and wandered back to the window. "As I was saying about the " "Yes, Martin, I remember you were talking about rain. Do you have something really important to say about rain? I thought the subject had been exhausted centuries before you were born." "I was going to remark that it is very late in the fall for such a heavy storm. It is almost cold enough to snow." "And that's that," Lynn commented indifferently. "A most sage observation." "I believe that's lightning," Martin continued, looking out over the lights of Manhattan Island.

Just then, rain began to beat heavily on the windows of the Kennedy apartment and a moment later lightning flashed viciously across the dark sky. A distant roll of thunder followed. "That was close," Martin observed. "It certainly is queer-an electrical storm so late in the " "Yes, yes, Martin, I know. Next, you will assure me again that there is nothing to fear because a large modern house like this, scientifically, is one of the safest places in the world." "Well, it is, Lynn.' 

"Whereas," Lynn resumed, "I am only concerned with whether or not Blair and Gerry were caught in the storm." "They probably hopped into a taxi.” 3

"I hope so."

Martin picked up the Evening Express and glanced idly at the 

"I'm waiting to be entertained," Lynn announced brightly. "What happened today?" 

Martin's face lighted up with a glowing smile. "Say, I forgot to tell you, Lynn. Steel went up!" "And did we make a lot of money?" Lynn inquired quickly, “because I really must have a new coat this winter. That old cat I've been wearing for three seasons is falling apart.' 

"Well, no," Martin responded reluctantly. "It only gained an eighth." "What only gained an eighth?" Lynn demanded. "Oh, yes, I know. You were talking about the stock market again. An eighth, you say? Well, I suppose we are about thirty-four cents nearer to financial security in our old age, and I can go ahead and have the cat cleaned and sewn together for another year.' 

"Yes, but-," Martin persisted, "even a small gain is significant in view of the general condition of the list. It marks a trend, you see.' "A trend? Oh, yes, that reminds me. I have been meaning to speak to you about it. I wish for one night you would forget your old statistics and the laws of probability. You might play a little more competently and anyway the world of mathematics is a dull subject to most human beings. Besides, bridge is supposed to be a game." "A scientific game," Martin amended. "Possibly so, but I wish you would try to remember what your cold logic did for us last Tuesday at the Turners' when you tried to make a small slam in clubs on the king, jack and one, just because the cards had been running their way for an hour and old Archimedes or somebody whispered in your ear that, mathematically, my hand must be a powerhouse, even though I didn't bid it."

"You have to gamble a bit now and then, Lynn. You may remember that our luck did change just after that." "Pure chance, Martin. Pure chance. Tonight, you bid and I'll take care of mine." "Now, Lynn-" your hands Whatever Martin had intended to say was lost. For, as a tremendous thunderbolt echoed across the skies, the electricity failed and plunged the apartment into darkness. "Lights gone," Martin remarked, quite unnecessarily. "Might be just a fuse, though."

He fumbled in his coat pocket and finally located his cigaret lighter, then stepped to the window, drew aside the drape, and looked out. "Gone across the court, too," he reported. "I don't care if they are out all the way to Hoboken, Martin, if you will just light the candles." "Fine idea, keeping candles on the mantelpiece. I hadn't thought about them."

Martin moved across the livingroom and lighted two large candles over the fireplace. "Science slips again," Lynn remarked caustically.

The candlelight sent a warm glow across the livingroom and Martin settled back on the sofa again. "Probably only be off for a few minutes," he said. "We rarely have very long interruptions of service in New York City." "Just the same, Martin, I'm glad they're large candles that will burn for hours, if we need them." "Of course we won't need them, but they are good insurance." "Insurance is a form of business, isn't it, Martin?" "Why, yes-" "That's all I wanted to know." "Now, Lynn, you don't understand. We're dependent upon business for our living." 5

"Such as it is, Martin. Such as it is. But do you suppose that business would mind if we didn't talk about it all the time every evening?" "See here-"

Again Martin was interrupted as the doorbell rang. 

Martin took one of the candles from the mantel, crossed the livingroom, and walked through the long, narrow foyer to the door. As he swung open the door, the candle sent a faint ray of light out into the black corridor. "Hello," Martin called cheerily. "This is devotion, darling," Blair Young replied, stepping into the doorway. "When I catch my breath, I'll have more to say about that a lot more." "Yeah," Gerry West added from the dark hall. "Eight floors are a lot of steps to climb to see anyone." 

"Why, you poor dears," Lynn exclaimed, rushing out into the foyer. "I hadn't thought about the elevators being cut off, too." "They require power," Martin observed. "Of course, dear," his wife answered, smiling. "You're always right about that sort of thing. You know so much more than I do." "It was a dark climb," Blair said. "Martin, you might at least have had the consideration to meet us halfway with that candle. I bruised my shins on every second step all the way." "And I felt like an Alpinist," Gerry added. "I lost hold of the railing at every landing and I could almost feel the bottom beckoning. It was a long way down after the first few flights. You might have had a Saint Bernard meet us at about the fifth floor with the customary succor for weary travelers." "I guess he means he's thirsty again," Lynn interpreted. "It's a wet night," Martin remarked. "I hope so," Gerry ventured. "Come on in, darlings," Lynn said. "Martin, dear, please get

busy in the kitchen before Gerry has to come right out and ask for a drink." "All right," Martin agreed. "What will you have-Scotch, Rye, Bourbon, Gin, Sherry 

?" "Do you really have all those!" Gerry interrupted. “I've been wasting my time in bars. Make mine Scotch." "I would like a nice strength-giving tonic," Blair said. "Brandy?" Martin suggested. "Splendid."

Martin disappeared into the kitchen and the others took chairs in the livingroom. "It's so nice you could come, Gerry," Lynn said with a smile, "and you, too, of course, Blair." "It's always a pleasure to be invited here," Gerry replied grandly. "Besides," Blair added coldly, "we see so little of Martin." "Yes, the poor dear," Lynn cooed, "he works so hard. Why, just before you came I was pleading with him to take things a little easier and have some fun, but he is such a responsible sort! Gerry, you're looking well tonight." "It's only his new tie," Blair explained. "He told me it was a gift, and I can believe that because it's easily the most expensive one 

I've ever seen him wear.' 

Gerry reddened a trifle, but added easily: "It's a beauty all right. Very cosy, this candlelight." "Isn't it, though?" Blair agreed. "It makes me feel all belle-like and romantic." "Nonsense," Lynn retorted. "That's the effect Gerry has on one." "Not at all, Lynn. Reporters are less than dust under my feet.

They're undependable. Now, give me a nice, fat, rich importer, and 

I could really get droopy." "You're libeling a great and honorable profession, Blair," Gerry

objected. "What do importers-rich or poor-have that reporters lack?" "Stability, manners, good breeding-and money, just to mention a few sterling qualities," Blair answered. "Reporters don't need money," Gerry answered defensively. "They have influence." "Yes, with desk sergeants, bartenders, telephone operators, and other important people of that general character," Blair suggested scornfully. "A nice, fat importer knows the people who matter because they always think he is going to get them something duty free or some scandalous French lingerie for their girl friends at half price." "A very materialistic point of view," Gerry commented loftily. "It is better than sitting around Sullivan Street caves discussing art over bum gin with reporters, who always owe their week's salary to the Italian bartender three days before they get it." "I haven't had a drink of gin since Repeal," Gerry averred, "and, besides, I'm always broke at least six days before I get paid." "You win, then, I guess," Lynn said. "I can see Blair's point, however. It all depends on the kind of girl." 

Blair tapped the rug with a dainty toe and smiled sweetly.

Martin returned from the kitchen with a tray of drinks. 

Blair sipped hers and commented graciously, "With two or three of these I could almost like reporters." "Blair," Gerry announced, "honesty compels me to say that with six of these very fine Scotches inside me I could almost-but not quite-be friends with that ugly marmoset that infests your apartment." "That's about the way I feel about little Melinda, too,” Lynn agreed. "Melinda's sweet," Blair replied. “It's an educated taste.” "Well, I guess Bryan was wrong after all. There must be something to this evolution business," Gerry said. "Say, did I ever tell

you about the old gal up in Yorkville, who kept twenty-seven cats in a small two-room apartment?" "No, and I wouldn't be interested in hearing about it," Blair answered firmly. "It was a swell story," Gerry continued. "I turned out a nice feature piece on it that the desk liked, but those so-and-sos out in the composing room buried it back on the real estate page." "Yes, I know," Blair cut in, "and practically nobody saw your byline." "I remember reading it, Gerry," Lynn said. "I thought it had real feeling. Don't you remember, Martin? I remarked about it." "Can't recall just now, Lynn."

Outside the apartment house rain continued to beat down in torrents and less frequent flashes of lightning preceded sharp peals of thunder. "I hope Melinda isn't frightened," Blair said. "She's reveling in it," Gerry responded. "It probably reminds her of the old days in the jungle—or was Melinda born in a First Avenue pet shop?" "You're impossible, Gerry." "Well," Lynn interrupted, "did we get together to play bridge or to discuss Melinda's state of health? Get the bridge table out, will you, Martin?" "Certainly, dear. Where is it?" "Don't ever acquire a husband, Blair," Lynn counseled. "They never know where anything is, especially if it means moving fifteen feet. We have lived in this apartment for two years and the bridge table has never been kept anywhere except in that foyer closet. 

Wait, Martin, I'll help you," she called, as Martin started across the livingroom to the foyer. "I can do it," Martin assured her, swinging open the closet door. 

They heard Martin rummaging around. "Bring a candle," he called. "The table is caught on something."

"Why, it can't be," Lynn shouted, jumping up from her chair. "There's nothing in there for it to catch on." "But it is, nevertheless," Martin answered. "All right," Lynn sighed, raising her eyebrows at Gerry with a look that clearly said, "You see how it is, and what I have to endure."

She took a candle from the mantel and went to the closet door. 

The candle sent only a faint, eerie light into the dark clothes closet, but a flash of lightning made it as bright as day. Almost with the flash, Martin recoiled in horror, his face ghastly. Lynn turned green. 

A gurgling sound came from Lynn's throat. With a quick motion, she slammed the door shut and the candle toppled from her hand as she shrank back against Martin and steadied herself against his shaking body. The candle rolled a few feet along the hardwood floor and went out. "There's a man in there," she gasped uncertainly. "He's dead," Martin added without conviction.

Gerry rushed out into the foyer. "Are you both crazy?" he demanded. "I-I-I mean it," Lynn stammered. "There-there is a man in there." "He's dead," Martin echoed. "And how do you know?" Gerry demanded in a matter-of-fact tone. "Dead?" Blair cried out in the livingroom. "My god-dead!"

Gerry half carried Lynn back into the livingroom, his arm encircling her slender waist and her head resting heavily on his shoulder.

He sat her down gently in a corner of the sofa. She sank back into the pillows-her eyes wide with fright and horror, as she looked into his face. up 

Gerry patted her shoulder and said: "I'll get you some brandy, you poor kid." 10

"Here, drink this," he said, snatching Blair's glass from her hand.

Lynn's lips trembled as he held the glass while she took a large swallow. She put her hand over Gerry's and said in a whisper, "Don't leave me, darling." 

Blair heard and glanced at Martin to see if he had also, but Martin still stood in a trance in the doorway. Gerry patted Lynn's shoulder again, and turned back to Martin. "Now," he directed, "let's see what this is all about, Martin."

Dead men were no novelty to Gerry, but he had a reporter's curiosity about them. He went to the mantel and was about to take the second candle into the foyer when Lynn stopped him. "Don't take that candle, Gerry," she screamed. "Oh, all right,” Gerry agreed, and, striking a match, he went to recover the other candle from the foyer floor. He lighted it again and handed it to Martin. "Here, you hold the candle while I see what this fellow looks like." "Now, this is one of those times when you really miss electric lights," Blair remarked casually.

Gerry opened the closet door and looked down on a small, whiteshirted man, sitting grotesquely on the floor, with one leg doubled under him. His body leaned heavily against the bridge table at one side of the closet. That he was dead was apparent at a first glance. 

Gerry looked him over intently and asked Martin, over his shoulder, "Who is he?" "I don't know, Gerry." "Aw-take a good look, Martin. He's beyond biting you." 

Martin peered into the closet carefully. 

He shook his head slowly. "I never saw him before in my life, Gerry." "You are certain that you don't know him?" Martin insisted, the look of complete astonishment on his face visible even in the poor light. "This is no place to be running into complete strangers." 11

"I can't help that. I don't know who he is." "Now, see here, Martin," Gerry continued, settling back against the foyer wall and still gazing into the closet, "you can confide in me; I'm not the district attorney." "Thank goodness for that, at least," Blair commented from the livingroom. "Reporters play at being detectives enough as it is." "Only to get the story," Gerry answered from the foyer. “Anythis is no time to be discussing that." way, "Of course not," Blair assured him. “Go ahead and play detective." 

Gerry grunted and turned back to Martin: "You don't find dead strangers parked in your clothes closets often, do you?" "That sounds a lot like the cop on the beat to me," Blair commented with a faint chuckle. "Say, stay out of this, will you, Blair?" Gerry asked. "Now, Martin, think! Does this man belong to the house staff-a carpenter or painter or someone like that perhaps?"

Martin pondered the possibility and again studied the dead man as carefully as the flickering candle would permit. "Well, he might have been," Martin admitted, "but I don't ever recall having seen him around the building. I'm positive he's never been in this apartment to do work when I've been home." "Then, who the devil is he, and what's he doing here?" Gerry demanded. 

Martin's slow, "I don't know," appeared to annoy Gerry as he bent down to resume his minute inspection of the dead man. "There's no blood here," he announced.

Then, as unexpectedly as they had gone out, the lights went on again, flooding the foyer and livingroom with most welcome illumination. "I'd forgotten there was such a thing as electricity," Blair remarked with a sigh. 12

Gerry saw that the man was short and thin and wiry looking. He wore neither shoes nor coat and his trousers, while well pressed, were made of a shoddy material. His arms hung loosely at his sides; his head was flung back and braced against the closet wall. 

His white shirt gleamed immaculately, but his blue flowered tie was of the type that curb peddlers sell for twenty-five cents down in the financial district. 

The man appeared to be between forty and forty-five. His hair was brown and curly, thinly streaked with gray. His eyes were open, and bulged pale blue and watery. The nose was heavy, the lips thin, the chin long and slender. Stained, crooked teeth, protruding from his open mouth, gave the face a ghastly appearance. 

Despite the horror written in the dead man's face, Gerry gazed on without concern. "Still no idea?" he asked at last. "None," Martin replied and Gerry closed the door. “Do you think he was murdered?" "That would be my guess, Martin. We can't do anything more here. Let's go inside and have a council of war. We have a few things to think about and talk over before the boys in blue come tearing in here to shake their fingers under our noses and to suggest that this gent is-excuse me, was-your father-in-law or an unnecessary second cousin that you wanted to bump off." "The sergeant speaking," Blair said coldly. "But," Martin protested, "I understood that the first thing to do in case of murder was to phone the police.' 

"Don't you ever read any mystery stories?" Gerry inquired caustically. "Well, not very often," Martin admitted. "No, of course you don't, or you would know enough not to phone the police." "Why not, Gerry?" "So all those at the party can sit down and discuss the murder. 13

That makes it more difficult for the police to solve the crime and gives the culprit a chance to slip the bloody dagger into the coat pocket of his rival for the hand of the banker's daughter." "Stop the nonsense, Gerry," Blair suggested, "and let's try to be intelligent." "Right, Blair," Gerry agreed. "For once, I'm more than a curious spectator at a murder." "We all are," Blair amended. "I don't like it," Lynn murmured. "Does anyone?" Blair snapped. "Proceed, sergeant." 

Gerry thought for a few minutes and then announced briskly: "First, we must find out who this fellow was. Did the face mean anything to you, Lynn?" 

More composed, Lynn answered calmly: "I didn't really see him, Gerry." "You'll have to look again then. And you, too, Blair." 

Once more the closet door was swung open and a bridge lamp directed at the features of its staring occupant. Blair took a quick look, muffled a scream and turned away. Lynn gazed at the dead man for a long time and seemed hesitant about speaking. Finally, she, too, turned away, shaking her head, "He's no one that I know." "Could he be a member of the house staff?" Gerry suggested hopefully again. "I don't think so." "Can you help, Blair?" "That's a face I would never forget, if I had ever seen it." "It's distinctive," Gerry agreed. "What now, sergeant?" Blair prodded. "Say, Lynn," Gerry said, as if an idea had just struck him, "were you at home all day?” "No, I wasn't." 

Gerry's face brightened, his black eyes gleamed. "So-," he said emphatically. 14

"Very professional, Sarge," Blair remarked. "Tell him it ain't so, Lynn." "Behave yourself, Blair," Gerry commanded. "This is important. Tell me what you did today, Lynn; that may provide the answer." 

Lynn began carefully: "Well, it wasn't what you would call a very eventful day—in view of the culmination. Martin left for his office about eight-thirty. 

I picked up the breakfast dishes and finished reading the paper. At nine, Gloria Brown, the colored girl who cleans for me, came in." "Does she have a key to the apartment?" Gerry interrupted. "Yes." "Now, we're getting somewhere. But-go on. Did you leave her alone here today?" "No, she left at noon." "And what did you do all morning yourself?" "I wrote a couple of letters, read a bit and sewed some buttons on Martin's shirts. How I hate sewing on buttons!" "Does it give you homicidal tendencies?" Blair asked sweetly. "Please shut up, Blair," Gerry ordered. "What about this afternoon, Lynn?" "I went to the movies-the Criterion. And I don't recommend the picture, if anyone asks you." "Why, darling," Martin interrupted, "you didn't say anything about that to me." "Martin, darling," Lynn replied with a smile, "I often go to movies without telling you.' 

"I don't care if you sat through the feature twice," Gerry continued, "what happened after that?" "There isn't much more, Gerry. I returned here about fourthirty and cooked dinner. Martin came in at six-thirty. We had a cocktail and then dinner." "Did you happen to look into that foyer closet?" 15

"I haven't been near it all day, Gerry-for which I'm now very thankful, but Gloria was in there this morning poking around to get the vacuum cleaner. If there had been any spooks hanging around, I'm positive that I would have heard about them very promptly. I imagine Gloria never gets any closer to a dead man than the pictures in the Daily Graphic." "Any visitors or strange telephone calls?" "No. Marian Curtis called, but that is hardly unusual.” "When?" "Just before I left for the movies." "Why?" "She wanted a book that I had planned to lend her and expected to run down for it later in the afternoon. I told her that I was going out, but that I would leave the key with the elevator man in case she wanted to come in and help herself." "Ah!" Gerry sighed, "and did she?" "How relentless!" Lynn exclaimed admiringly. "The Sarge has to be on his toes," Blair contributed with a titter. "Well?" Gerry inquired. "Yes, she did. Charlie, the elevator man, told me when I returned from the movies that she had been down." "Did he say how long Marian stayed in the apartment?" "Now, why should I ask him a question like that, Gerry?" "You shouldn't have, but the police will want to know." "She's my closest friend, Gerry." "I know, but the police won't care if she's your Aunt Minnie." "Speaking of the police," Martin broke in. "Don't you think we should call them?" "I suppose so, Martin. We don't seem to be making any progress. It just gets more complicated." "Giving up the case, Sarge?" Blair inquired. 

Ignoring Blair's quip, Gerry turned to Martin: "Do you want me to handle this?" 16

"If you will." "All right. I know Captain Wyckoff fairly well. He's the Leander Wyckoff who ran down the thugs who did all those night club jobs. He's chief of the Homicide Squad for this section of the city and a real detective. He's almost too bright to be a cop." "What a reporter he would be," Blair said with a grin. "Save it," Gerry replied with a smile and started for the telephone.

Silence fell over the other three as they waited for Gerry to get his connection. Listening eagerly, they heard him give his name and add, "of the Express." In the quiet they heard an audible click as the call was plugged through the switchboard at the Centre Street 

Headquarters. 

"Hello, Captain Wyckoff, this is Gerry West of the Express. I am at 706 East Fifty-fifth Street at the apartment of friends. The name is Kennedy-Martin Kennedy, apartment 8B. We have found a man dead in a clothes closet."

Gerry stopped talking and listened impatiently. The others could hear a staccato bass on the other end of the line. Apparently Captain 

Wyckoff was talking in loud, angry tones. Gerry interrupted him. "Skip it, Captain," he broke in. "I'm neither drunk nor disorderly. Besides, you shouldn't use that sort of language over the telephone. One of the operators might get the wrong idea. . . .” ...» "All right, all right, steady now," he resumed after a pause. "I'm trying to give it to you as fast as you can take it. Here it is in the proverbial nutshell: Half an hour ago a friend and I came over to the Kennedys' apartment to play some bridge. We talked for a while, like polite people, before sitting down to the real business of the evening, which is wolfing one another's hearts out for a tenth of a cent a point." "Yeah, a big game. "> "No, we don't usually play for such heavy stakes, but today was pay day. But to get back to the deceased. My host, Mr. Kennedy, 17

opened the door of a clothes closet and there was the dead fellow parked in there where his bridge table should have been." "No, I didn't look to see if the table was still there. We have been too busy trying to figure out who the blighter is. Nobody here knows him." 

And then after another break. "It's curious all right. Mr. Kennedy isn't the type that goes around killing people and stowing them away in closets just so he can act surprised when he finds them again." "No, sir, I didn't notice anyone sneaking into that closet to commit suicide either, not since I have been here. We were only four originally and we are still all here. . . ." "Another thing, Captain. This is a very peculiar corpse. There isn't blood so far as I can see. any • · · 

"Yes, he's dead all right. He isn't just asleep or dazed, I can assure you." "You will come up yourself? Fine! I appreciate that a lot, Captain. Incidentally, as a personal favor, don't bring the entire department on the first trip, and forget those eggs down in the press room, if your conscience will permit."

Gerry hung up the receiver and sauntered back into the livingroom, visibly gayer as the result of his conversation with the Captain. "The Captain's a fine fellow," he reported. "Thought I was drunk when I first talked with him. Police become callous about murder; they see so much of it." "The Captain sounds very human to me," Blair said. 

The smile disappeared from Gerry's face, and he asked quickly: "Lynn, I just thought. What about that maid? She had a key to the apartment and that looks like the first answer to me. You can't walk a man through a locked door in order to murder him on the inside. What do you know about her?" 18

"Too little, I'm afraid. She was recommended to us by a friend.

We've had her about six months. She's quiet and terrifying-looking, but a pleasant soul; about the usual Harlem type, I'd say." "Know her address?" "No, but she works for other people here in the building and one of them or the manager might. I might add that she dislikes Father 

Divine and plays the policy game regularly and passionately." "That would cover a thousand—”

The apartment bell interrupted. "That's the Captain. I'll let him in."

At the door they heard him say, "It was good of you to come yourself, Captain. And you, too," he added as the gigantic Detective Sergeant Michael Kearney stepped in after Captain Wyckoff. "It's all part of the job, West," Captain Wyckoff said pleasantly. "Sorry I got you wrong over the phone at first. Drunken reporters bother me to death that way every once in a while."

They walked into the livingroom and the other three arose from their chairs. Fascinated, Lynn, Blair, and Martin kept their eyes glued on the Captain. He looked astonishingly little like a detective. He was tall, easily above six feet, and so stooped that he appeared taller than he was. His face was thin and finely-chiseledthe kind of face that grew old gracefully. His eyes had laugh wrinkles around them. He limped slightly and carried a knotted and competent-looking cane that would have made a very respectable weapon.

His eyes, however, were his most compelling characteristic and drew attention sharply away from the rest of his features. Martin found it difficult to look straight into them, yet there was a magnetlike quality about them which would not permit him to shift his gaze. There was nothing unusual about their steely blue color or the protecting maze of bushy black eyebrows above them. While 19

not unfriendly-looking, they had depth and coldness. The left one had a whitish cast that seemed to give it a curiously intense expression as the Captain glanced quickly from face to face.

Sergeant Kearney appeared to be the perfect foil for his superior. 

Large and Irish, he fitted the average citizen's conception of a policeman to the last detail-from his big red nose down to his number twelve shoes.

Gerry introduced Captain Wyckoff and Sergeant Kearney and explained briefly about the finding of the body. The two police officers knelt down in front of the foyer closet and the Sergeant turned a powerful flashlight on the body. Without touching the dead man, Captain Wyckoff peered intently at him for several minutes. Then he gave the Sergeant a quizzical glance.

Clearly, the Sergeant understood this question without words for he answered soberly: "Plenty stiff, Captain, and hours ago.' >"> "The Sergeant's an expert on dead bodies," the Captain explained as he straightened up, and closed the closet door. "One look and he can guess with reasonable accuracy just about how long a man has been dead. You should have been a medical examiner, Mike." "Them bums," the Sergeant grunted.

The Kennedys and Blair were to learn, as Gerry already knew, that the Sergeant was a man of few words and almost no facial expressions, but many suspicions, which was quite natural when suspicions were his bread and butter. 

Captain Wyckoff addressed the Sergeant again without more than a nod of his head, and promptly the Sergeant answered: "Some bum stopped his clock about five hours ago." "Now, that is discouraging," the Captain said genially. "At least we know, to begin with, that whoever did this job has the customary head start on us. It's trying; we never get a fair start." 

Captain Wyckoff's apparently casual and informal approach to the business of trapping criminals was even more puzzling than 20

his benign appearance. In the next instant, however, he demonstrated that beneath his friendly exterior he was a person of welltempered steel.

Even before he spoke, Martin felt the full power of the Captain's eyes. Try as he would, he could not wriggle free from their icy stare and confusion overcame him as he heard the Captain ask sharply: "Mr. Kennedy, what did you do with the bludgeon?" "Why-why," Martin stammered, "why, I don't know."

Lynn and Blair squirmed uncomfortably in their chairs, their voices entirely lost for the moment.

Gerry eyed the Captain casually, however, and said easily, "Captain, I told you over the phone that Mr. Kennedy isn't the killer type." 

Released by the Captain's eyes, Martin managed to answer: "I don't know what you are talking about, Captain. There wasn't any weapon here so far as I know." "Forgive me," the Captain replied with a slow smile, once again the debonair gentleman who seemed to hunt killers only as a sideline. "I didn't mean to be so rude. Of course, there was no weapon, or we would have been told of it at once." 

The Sergeant grimaced. "Well, we must move along," the Captain continued briskly. "The medical examiner is on his way up here, and our customary procedure is under way. Next, I must ask some questions. Suppose we start with you, Mr. Kennedy."

Cold sweat stood out on Martin's forehead, but the Captain kept his eyes roving slowly around the apartment as he continued: "Briefly, where have you been and what did you do today?" "There isn't much to report, Captain. I left for my office about eight-thirty as usual, stayed there all day except for luncheon from one until two and returned here at six-thirty for dinner. Afterward, I sat reading until Miss Young and Mr. West came in about nine to play bridge." 21

"Yes, yes," the Captain answered impatiently, "West told me about that over the phone. Nothing out of the ordinary all day?" "No, sir." "No idea who this man was?" he asked with a nod toward the foyer. "None." "Then how in the world can you account for his presence here?" “I can't.” "Any enemies who might want to cause you trouble?" "None who would go to that extreme, I'm certain." "It's extreme all right," the Captain agreed.

Again he asked the Sergeant a question without asking one. "It don't add up," the Sergeant answered, and the Captain nodded agreement. "The Sergeant is disturbed," he explained, as if the explanation were an afterthought, "because it is not usual to have identification troubles-unless the murdered individual is the victim of gangsters. 

Of course, that may be what you have here. What is your work, by the way, Mr. Kennedy?" "Accountant, with Pedrick and Blinken, public accountants." "Oh, yes, and now your story, please, Mrs. Kennedy?"

Lynn repeated the account of her day that she had given before the police were called. As Gerry had predicted, Marian Curtis' call seemed to interest the Captain. "Why didn't Mrs. Curtis come in for the book at once when you told her that you were going out?" "She explained that she was not dressed." "Why didn't you take it up to her then?" "That wasn't necessary," Lynn explained. "I've left the key with the elevator man for her on other occasions." "Often?" the Captain asked. "This had happened several times before?" "Yes." 22

"Suppose you telephone Mr. and Mrs. Curtis and ask them to step down, will you?" "What shall I tell them?" Lynn inquired. "Just say they are needed here at once." "That seems unnecessarily cruel," Lynn protested. “It would frighten Marian to death." "All right," the Captain agreed complacently. "If it's any easier, tell them you've found a dead man in a clothes closet."

Lynn seemed relieved as she arose to telephone. She dialed the number and waited while the bell rang several times. Finally, Marian answered. "Marian, this is Lynn.' 

Her voice was low, but distinct. "You and George must come down here right away," she continued. 

She paused and then went on: "A man has been found dead in our foyer closet." 

She listened again. "Now don't be upset, Marian. It's nothing that we have anything to do with; we don't even know the man. Nobody recognizes him! 

Anyway, the police want to talk with you and George."

After another brief pause, she added, "Yes, because you were down here in the apartment this afternoon to get a book." 

Lynn was silent for a few seconds and then she gasped, "He isn't? 

Where in the world is he? .. When?” She hung up and stood for a moment beside the telephone table before returning to the livingroom. "A surprise?" the Captain inquired quietly. "Mrs. Curtis will be down in a few minutes-as soon as she can put on some clothes. Mr. Curtis is out of town." 

Sergeant Kearney swung around with a speed that belied his weight and demanded: "When did he take it on the lam?" 23

ear: "Watch a real sergeant work, "This evening," Lynn said quietly. 

Blair whispered in Gerry's reporter." 24

CHAPTER 

2 N ATMOSPHERE of extreme expectancy settled over the 

Kennedys' livingroom. Blair sat rigidly in her chair. Martin

fumbled with the buttons on his vest. Gerry stalked about the room, puffing furiously on a cigaret. Lynn toyed nervously with the heavy gold necklace about her throat, sometimes drawing it tight enough to almost choke her as she wound it about her cold fingers. 

In the silence that followed Marian Curtis' strange news over the telephone only Captain Wyckoff appeared entirely imperturbed.

Even Sergeant Kearney seemed to feel that sinister forces were at work for he sat gingerly on the edge of a lounging chair and kept his head cocked slightly to one side as if listening intently for something.

In an orderly fashion, the detective captain proceeded with the questioning of Blair and Gerry. He obtained no information of apparent worth. Blair had spent the day in her West Ninth Street studio working on a picture. Gerry had worked all day at the 

Express and, although he had been out of the office to cover an assignment, there was no indication that he had been near the Kennedy apartment.

Satisfied, Captain Wyckoff sank back in an easy chair and closed 25

his eyes. To the amazement of the Kennedys and Blair, his breathing immediately became audibly heavy and regular. A moment later, his head slipped forward slightly, and they knew for certain that the Captain had gone to sleep. 

Gerry smiled and whispered, "It's an old trick of his. He can sleep anywhere at any time." 

The Sergeant scowled and held a finger to his lips. There was silence again.

Lynn began to appear nervous and excited. As time passed, it was clear that Marian was not hurrying the matter of dressing. Lynn arose and walked to the entrance to the foyer, and stood there. Little wrinkles in her smooth white forehead suggested that her mind was deep in thought.

More minutes passed, and finally Marian Curtis rang the apartment bell. Lynn wheeled and went swiftly to the door. "Marian," she whispered, "no one recognizes him." 

Marian looked straight into her eyes, and Lynn stared back with a look in her large, brown ones that seemed to spell out "s-t-e-a-d-y." 

Probably words would have been unnecessary, but in any event there was no time for them, for the Sergeant had been at Lynn's elbow almost from the second she had started toward the door. He escorted the two women back into the livingroom with a wave of his hand.

The Captain was awake and on his feet. He nodded pleasantly to 

Marian and introduced himself: "Sorry to inconvenience you, Mrs. Curtis. I thought you might be able to help us." 

Again Martin saw those eyes grow piercingly sharp as the Captain studied Marian. He saw an attractive ash-blonde in her early thirties an outdoor woman with a solid athletic figure topped by a delicate face. She was the kind of woman that you met out on the seventh tee instead of at the bridge tables on the clubhouse veranda. 26

Perhaps her most appealing characteristic was her air of simple dignity. "Mrs. Kennedy has told you why I asked you to come down here," the Captain said. "I must ask you to look at the man. So, if you don't mind . . .” "Well," Marian faltered. "Well, if I must . . ." "Please, this way."

The Captain led her to the foyer closet. Lynn took hold of 

Marian's forearm so firmly that her fingernails seemed to be digging into the bare skin. The door creaked a little as the Captain opened it, and Lynn's grip on Marian's arm tightened. Little white spots were visible around her fingertips. eyes Marian gave a little cry-a bare gasp, nothing more. They stood there for a moment only. Marian looked up at the Captain, her wide open and her face pale except for two red spots burning on her cheeks. "I have never seen a dead person before," she explained simply. "Ever see this man alive then?" the Captain asked swiftly. "No," Marian replied.

The Captain closed the door with a bang. Lynn and Marian kept their eyes averted from each other. Before Captain Wyckoff could proceed, the doorbell rang again. It was Dr. Himber, the medical examiner.

The doctor was a short, fat man. He had a small waxed mustache of reddish hue and wore thick glasses. His face looked mussed up. 

The Captain and Dr. Himber spoke in low voices. They walked to the foyer closet together and the doctor bent over the dead man for some time. He straightened up and reported to the Captain: "There is a bad bruise at the base of the skull. I think that might be it. Of course, I can't tell positively until I've made a more complete examination. I have the wagon out front." "I could have a report by morning?" the Captain suggested. 27

"By all means. I'll get right to work," he replied, with more good cheer than the situation appeared to call for in the opinion of the Kennedys and their guests. "There may be nothing more. One good hard blow in that area would be plenty to kill any man. It looks like a murder to me." "Now, don't get yourself out on a limb," the Captain cautioned with a laugh. "You medical examiners put Caspar Milquetoast to shame at times." "Ah, but we have to be so very careful," Dr. Himber answered, as if each word were an important pronouncement. “A fall on an icy sidewalk might look exactly the same as this case, for instance." "This fellow hasn't been here since last winter," the Captain remarked. "At least, I don't think he has. How about getting your routine under way?" "Routine, bah!" the medical examiner snorted. "What a name for science!"

He ambled to the apartment door and called to a policeman outside: "Send the boys up, Joe." 

Captain Wyckoff laughed and returned to the livingroom. "With your permission," he said, "I'm going to shift our little meeting here to the bedroom. This place will be filled soon with 

Dr. Himber's men to remove the body, our police photographer, and the fingerprint experts. They will be busy here for some time and time is important to all of us."

When they reassembled in the bedroom, the Captain resumed: "Mrs. Curtis, we are still waiting to hear what, if anything, you can tell us that may help. To begin with, will you tell us at what time you were in this apartment this afternoon and just how long you remained here?" 

Marian answered without a pause—as if she had been expecting the exact question: "It was just about three-thirty this afternoon. I found the book for which I came and looked through a stack of 28

magazines for one I wanted. I could not have been here more than five minutes at the very most." "You locked the door when you left?" "Yes, the door has a double lock. It locks automatically when the door is closed and is double locked by turning the key. I know that Mrs. Kennedy does that when she goes out, so I did, too.” "You are certain that you did this afternoon?" "Positive." "How would you explain the fact that someone, perhaps several persons, entered this apartment after you left and before four-thirty when Mrs. Kennedy returned?" "I couldn't-unless they had a key. Perhaps they had a skeleton or a master key," Marian suggested. "can "Forgetting that possibility for the time," the Captain continued, be certain that the key you used didn't get into the possession of another person?" you "Of course, I can't, Captain, but I returned it to Charlie, the regular day elevator man, when I went back up to my apartment. 

I believe Mrs. Kennedy will assure you that he is a very reliable person." "He certainly is," Lynn volunteered. "I have complete confidence in Charlie." "He wouldn't permit another person to use the key unless authorized by you, Mrs. Kennedy?” "Not under any circumstances. The house rules forbid it." "Even a friend? Someone who came here fairly frequently and was well known to Charlie as a friend of yours or Mr. Kennedy's?"

Martin searched back through his orderly mind for some explanation of the Captain's line of questioning. The thought struck him at once that the Captain was convinced that some one of their friends was the murderer. "Never," Lynn was answering the Captain. "Charlie is sixty and 29

German-born. Most of his years have been spent in cultivating a sense of responsibility. Besides, he has a passionate devotion to rules and regulations." "We must talk with Charlie soon," the Captain suggested in an aside to Sergeant Kearney. "Suppose I tell the boys to drag the manager out of his hole," the Sergeant answered. "That won't be necessary, I'm certain," Martin spoke up. "He's probably out in the corridor this very minute blaspheming the Kennedys for bringing disaster on his head. Policemen spell disaster in his language. The owners will crucify him in the morning.' "Yes, he isn't supposed to allow murderers to run wild around here," Lynn added with an hysterical laugh. "Neither are we, Mrs. Kennedy," the Captain said reprovingly. "We'll go on here, Sergeant, and let the manager brood for a while.

Now then, Mrs. Curtis, you noticed nothing irregular in the apartment this afternoon?" "Nothing." "You had no occasion to look into that foyer closet?" "No."

Then, like a rapier thrust in the midst of his slow, informal questioning, the Captain flashed, "Where did you say your husband went?" "I didn't say," Marian answered evenly. "You didn't ask.” "That's right," the Captain admitted smoothly, "but now I will ask that very question, please.' "He went to Chicago this afternoon on the four-thirty-two train." "Then, this was unusual?”

"They cleaned me out in '29 and again in '33 when I thought the firing was all over, but that was my fault. I should have stuck to police work; I know something about that.”

Captain Wyckoff was again the leisurely and expansive guest. 

There seemed to be less of the policeman about him than ever. "Was this trip of your husband's planned long in advance, Mrs. Curtis?" "No, it was a rather sudden decision, I believe." 

Sergeant Kearney broke into a smile again, and whipped a notebook out of his hip pocket. Solemnly he wet the lead of a battered pencil and made an entry in a careful scrawl.

The Captain did not seem to notice, but remarked sympathetically: "That's the way with business trips usually, Mrs. Curtis. Can you think of anything else that might help us, or have we about covered the ground?" "No, I am afraid not, Captain. I guess that I haven't been of any real assistance." 31

"Only time will tell as to that, Mrs. Curtis."

After the Sergeant closed the door into the livingroom, the Captain again sank into a chair. Evidently he intended to go to sleep again.

"Well, if this is supposed to be a great thriller, you must excuse me for not breaking down entirely." "It has possibilities," the Captain replied calmly. "Now . . ."

The Captain smiled. "Sure, West. I'm learning the ropes finally. The rest of you stay here, please. They'll insist upon viewing the scene of the crime unless the breed has changed overnight."

A few minutes later they heard the Captain talking with the reporters in the livingroom. Sergeant Kearney kept himself busy. 

With no effort to disguise his purpose, he examined every inch of the bedroom and the bath leading from it. He opened clothes closets and ransacked them, examined each dress of Lynn's and every suit of Martin's, peeked into hat boxes and got down on his hands and knees to scrutinize the floor and to peer under the beds. Taylor Eubank admitted them and showed them to chairs in the livingroom. Only a very red nose and a damp handkerchief, held gingerly in her hand as if poised for a hurry-up trip to her nose, lent a discordant note. "My wife has a cold," Taylor Eubank explained quite unnecessarily, and Sergeant Kearney mumbled something that sounded like, "I'll say she is."

It seemed to be in answer to something that Lea had said a moment earlier out in the corridor. "In that event we're very sorry to have intruded," the Captain apologized and, after explaining why they were there, added, "we'll make it as brief as possible." "A murder next door!" Mrs. Eubank exclaimed. "How dreadful! I wouldn't have been home today, if I'd known that. I feel so sorry for Mr. and Mrs. Kennedy." 43

CHAPTER 

3 YNN awakened with a tired, sick feeling.

The soft autumn sun- shine blurred her sleepy eyes. She glanced at the clock and, as her eyes became accustomed to the sun, she saw that it was nine. She reached over to nudge Martin; he was late for the office already. With her hand in mid-air, she remembered and sank back in bed. This was the morning after the murder-the murder in their apartment.

At that very instant, the thought occurred to her, hundreds of thousands of work-bound New Yorkers were avidly snatching every known detail of that murder from their favorite morning newspapers. She could picture them without difficulty-clutching straps in smelly subway and elevated trains, standing in tube trains, buses, ferryboats, even street cars, reading laboriously and chewing nervously, finding a brief release in the troubles of other people before the daily grind began. She had seen them herself many times, but never before had she appreciated the agony of being the center of their curiosity.

She turned her eyes away from the sun and tried to go back to sleep. It was useless. In the long hours of the night she had made a decision. Now that daylight had come again, she wondered if her decision had been the right one. Right or wrong, however, she 47

knew that she must abide by it, irrespective of what the consequences might be. 

Martin stirred. "Martin," she called, "wake up!" 

Martin opened his drowsy eyes. "Is it late?" he asked quickly, shaking his head vigorously to awaken himself. "Yes, Martin, but it doesn't matter.”

Martin looked at his wife blankly, and then the memory of the night before dawned on him also. "That's so," he agreed, and closed his eyes again. "Martin, I feel so terrible," Lynn moaned. "If I could have breakfast in bed. . . ?" "Of course, dear," her husband answered, getting out of bed and into his bathrobe.

Nine floors above them, a sleepy detective, who stood outside apartment 17D, heard footsteps pacing back and forth, back and forth, as they had for hours. He took a notebook from his hip pocket and wrote: "9 a.m.-Mrs. Curtis didn't go to bed at all."

A mile away, an alarm clock clattered noisily. Gerry West reached out sleepily and turned it off, muttering: "They won't expect me today. Nobody expects me to be anywhere today except that slave-driving city editor-and he'll even expect me to cover my own trial when it comes along."

In the apartment immediately adjacent to the Kennedys', Dr. 

Hildegarde Ross lingered thoughtfully over her third cup of coffee. 48

"I must telephone the Captain this morning," she said to herself. 

On the same floor Mrs. Taylor Eubank stood in the doorway of her apartment, again clad in her canary-yellow outfit, and kissed her husband good-bye. "Luck," she called as he turned away to the elevator. "Keep the door locked, dear, and don't let any strangers in." 

Down in Greenwich Village, Blair Young slept on peacefully.

On an extra-fare train nearing Chicago, a Pullman porter was brushing off the coat of a rather portly, red-faced man whose hand shook as he handed the porter a half dollar. The brief case, in his other hand, was lettered in gold, "George V. Curtis.” 

Down in Police Headquarters on Centre Street, Captain Wyckoff, in fresh linen, was sipping a cup of black coffee at his desk. The office door opened and Dr. Himber walked in. "I thought I was the only night owl in this department," was the medical examiner's greeting. "But, here you are, and looking like a newborn baby too. Don't start this all night business at your age; you're much too young for it." 

The Captain smiled brightly. "Sit down, Doc. I don't do it usually, but this case looked like one of those things which must be worked out quickly." "They can usually wait, Captain." "That's easy for you to say, Doc. I've just finished reading your report. Nothing besides that blow at the base of the skull, is that correct? No other possible cause of death?" 49

"Nothing else. Somebody struck him with the usual blunt instrument and that was plenty. I'd say he never knew what hit him. He died almost instantaneously from the hemorrhage." The good Sergeant has been indulging in a bit of guessing again." "Guessing it may have been, but the Sergeant has a knack for it. 

It saved me a lot of time for him to say at nine-thirty last night that the man had been dead for around five to six hours. It took you all night to get around to almost agreeing with him." the confidence man. In appearance he suggested the Adolphe Menjou type for he was small and nattily dressed in the Broadway style.

His hair was black and heavily pomaded, and he wore the conventional waxed mustache. In looks he appeared well equipped to carry on his chosen profession, and it was easy to see why elderly widows and lonesome school teachers with a few thousands to invest in matrimony might find him appealing. "House, you look too prosperous," Captain Wyckoff announced after scrutinizing the confidence man for some time. "What racket are you swinging now?" "I'm taking a vacation, chief." "So, you plan to be difficult," the Captain said stiffly. "Maybe, Sergeant, the boys downstairs can lubricate his tongue a bit.” "None of that, Captain," House broke in jauntily. "You haven't a thing on me and you can't keep me here. You know that as well as I do." "It's murder this time, House, not peddling without a license." "Sure, it's murder," House responded with a dismissing wave of his hand, "when you find the candidate, but you can deal me out on this hand. There isn't anything sadistic about me. I take their money, sure, but I don't go around cracking heads just for the fun of it." "There's always a first time," the Captain commented, "especially with you love-'em-and-leave-'em experts.” "Now, Captain, don't get overheated," House advised. "The Sergeant here took me down to lamp that stiff. I don't know him. Never saw him before, in fact. Furthermore, I don't even know the Kennedys. Sure, when I saw the bulldog edition of the American last night I recalled having seen the skirt in the elevator, but I didn't even know her name." "Words and music familiar," the Captain observed, with a look of disgust on his face. "It's true enough to suit me," House answered defiantly. "You aren't the one who has to be satisfied, House." 62

"Write your own ticket then." "We usually do," the Captain said. "Where were you all of yesterday afternoon and last night?" "I have that ready," House responded genially. "I've done time and after the third stretch I began to wake up. Now I keep records." "You keep what?" "Records. Just to annoy the police with the truth now and then. 

Whenever a smell is raised for ten blocks around me in this or any other town, I know that I'll land for questioning sooner or later. It was sooner this time, Captain. In fact, the speed of your sergeant amazed me. It was the fastest that I'd ever been pulled in, even for jobs that I did." "Get to the point, House." "Oh, yes, the records. As I was saying, Captain, I keep a little book of my daily activities. Naturally, I got it right up to the minute when I read of last night's unfortunate occurrence."

The confidence man reached into his coat pocket and pulled out a little black notebook with a pencil attached to it. "I like this kind," he explained. "Never have to hunt around for a pencil." "Proceed, House!" "I write off every few hours at times like these," he continued. "Now, this is yesterday's layout." 

He opened the memorandum book and read: "Arose at eleven, bathed, dressed and cooked my own breakfast -bacon, eggs, two pieces of dry toast, and coffee au lait. Left the apartment at twelve-thirty and met Helen . . .” 

Here he looked up and explained: "She's in a show. Her real name is Kate Gillette, but she uses

Helen Devere on the stage. I've known her for a long time and she's as straight as they come." "Never mind the plug, House," the Captain said. "We form our own opinions about people." 63

House resumed reading without comment: 

"Took Helen to the Astor for luncheon and left there at twothirty. We went to a movie at the RKO, came out at five and went to Jack's for cocktails. We stayed on for dinner there-steak and 

French fries with a field salad, coffee and pumpkin pie. I left Helen at Broadway and Fiftieth Street at seven-thirty. I went directly to 

Jake's Billiard Parlors and stayed there until midnight. Then I dropped into Eddie's Coffee House and spent the rest of the night gassing with the boys there." "Sounds like Pepys Diary," the Captain remarked sarcastically. "Why all the menus?" "Police idea, not mine," House replied modestly. "They always ask fool questions like that, so I try to anticipate." "Very cooperative of you." "Isn't it?" "I suppose you're ready with all kinds of witnesses from the 

Astor, Jack's, the RKO, Jake's and Eddie's?" "I'm well known in those places," House answered quietly. "I guess that someone would recall having seen me around yesterday." "No doubt," the Captain observed. "Well, you seem to have equipped yourself with the usual ironclad alibi that you babies always have." "I guess it'll hold, if necessary, Captain." "You may want to use it at that," the Captain remarked indifferently. "How are you making a living these days? Lea tells me that you keep up a fairly expensive apartment." "Just a business investment, Captain. I have to have a good address." "I asked you what you were doing," the Captain continued impatiently. "That's a little secret, Captain, but I can tell you that it's something which would interest the Securities Exchange Commission more than it would any blue-jacketed policeman." 64

"Practically going straight and doing your burglarizing almost within the law," the Captain suggested. "Notice any difference?" "No, but I have my self respect." "That," Captain Wyckoff observed, "is just about as important to you as an electric refrigerator would be to an Eskimo. Well, what's keeping you?" "I can go?" House asked, an astonished look spreading slowly over his face. "Get out before I change my mind. Show him the way out, Sergeant." 

Mike Kearney returned to his superior's office a few minutes later to report: "Leary is on his tail. I wish you'd let me handle that little runt, Captain. I'd have taken some of that grease off his hair." "It's your turn next time he comes in, Mike. He'll be back all right. Can you figure out why he carries that little notebook around with him?" "Naw, I can't, Captain, unless the little squirt's tellin' the truth.

It's a new angle for a confidence man-and here I thought I knew the woiks. Usually, they don't want nothing atall left in writin'." "What happened down in the morgue?" "Well, foist, I lets him roost down there for a half hour or so. 

That helps to break them up; they usually get pretty sick in the belly by then and, if you put your man in the right corner, he usually lamps a lot of stiffs bein' carted around. The boys down there is always willin' to enter into the spirit of the occasion and help to key a man up. I hear the boys give a swell performance—one of the best." "And what?" "Absolutely no dice. After letting him soak up plenty of atmosphere and formaldehydie, I leads him over to the slab and pulls the sheet with a quick jerk. He don't let out a peep. He just leans over and looks at the corpse and says, 'Nope, I never seen him before. 65

What made you think I would recognize this stiff?' He ain't got no noives atall." "I'm not surprised about that, Mike. He might try to act nervous on purpose, if he wanted to mislead us, but he's an old hand. 

He knows all the usually-employed tricks as well as we do-probably better. What was it? Twenty-one arrests?" "Right, Captain, and in thirteen states." "With three convictions! He's seen the country, Sergeant, and has been worked on by better men than us, Mike. I his picture taken." suppose you had "Yeah, he objected some, though. He's a regular Sing Sing lawyer. Knows all his little rights and priv❜leges, but we took the pitchers 

anyway.' "Good." "I told him he could send his lawyer around for the fillums after the case was closed." "All right, then. Get after this alibi of his. It probably is air tight, but pay particular attention to the Helen Devere angle. Question her carefully and watch for any small slips or discrepancies.” "You mean mistakes, Captain?" "That's close enough. Have the photographer send me a print of that picture of House." "All right, Captain. See you later."

Captain Wyckoff sat at his desk, with closed eyes, for some fifteen minutes until a uniformed policeman entered and laid a picture of 

Jack House on his desk. "Sergeant said you wanted this." "Right. Send a car around, will you?"

The patrolman saluted and left. The Captain stuffed the picture into his pocket, put on his hat and topcoat, and walked out to the street. The sun was shining brightly but the air carried a very definite nip of autumn. 66

"Fine day for it," the Captain murmured gaily to himself. “I always have to be working on a case on a day like this." 

A Homicide Squad car drew up at the curb. "Kennedy apartment," the Captain directed briefly. 

Captain Wyckoff spent the trip uptown in silent admiration of the fall scene. Entering the Kennedys' apartment house, he rode to their floor, and paused outside their apartment to talk with the policeman on duty. "Anything new?" he asked. "Nothing, Captain. Kennedy went out a while ago-to work, I guess. One of the boys has him." "That's fine. Come in with me." 

Lynn admitted them. "My husband has gone to his office," she informed them at once.

She was wearing a black velvet housecoat wrapped tightly about her trim little figure. She was freshly made up and only the slightly purple smudges beneath her expressive eyes gave a hint of the fact that she hadn't rested well.

The Captain wondered if they'd been expected. He knew already that Lynn was the kind of woman who never let any man see her untidy. "I really came to see you, Mrs. Kennedy." "I'm just having coffee, Captain. Would you care for a cup?"

The Captain thanked her but declined and she went on sipping hers, quietly waiting for the Captain to speak. Instead, he continued to study her with his sharp eyes; the whitish cast seemed to move jerkily. 

Finally Lynn put down her cup and asked, "Have you learned anything about this terrible business yet?" "Oh, yes, Mrs. Kennedy. In fact, a number of very valuable things." "Who is he?" 67

"Unfortunately, that's not one of the things we've learned," the 

Captain answered smoothly.

For an instant an expression of relief cleared Lynn's face and lighted the tired circles under her eyes. Then, it was gone. "Mrs. Kennedy, do you know where your maid is?" "Why, no, Captain." "She should be here, shouldn't she?" "Yes. She's supposed to be here at nine."

The Captain's eyes narrowed and the cast seemed to vibrate as he asked: "Where is she?" "Does it matter particularly?" Lynn inquired. "It does." "Well, then I don't know just what to say," Lynn answered, with a helpless look in her eyes. "She might-she might be working for someone else this morning." "Does she do that without informing you in advance?" "Well, no-no, she doesn't-usually." "But she might have this morning, Mrs. Kennedy?" "It's a possibility, Captain." "Another morning it might have been," the Captain retorted coldly. "What do you mean, Captain?" Lynn demanded. "Just what I said—any other morning, but not this morning!" "I'm afraid I don't understand what you're talking about," Lynn answered with an appealing note in her voice. "We shall make it perfectly clear, then," the Captain replied patiently. "When did you last see Gloria?" "Early yesterday afternoon, as I told you last night." "You haven't talked with her on the telephone since then?" "No."

The expression of surprise on Lynn's face seemed most genuine, but the Captain studied her face carefully before continuing: 68

"Gloria disappeared about five o'clock yesterday afternoon." 

Lynn said nothing for a moment and then remarked slowly, "That's strange!" "Do you really think so?" "Of course," Lynn replied quickly. "There-there was no reason." "I don't see any facts," Lynn complained. "I don't see as many as I should," the Captain answered. "Some people don't tell me things they should." "Why, Captain I can't imagine anyone not confiding in you!

Somehow I feel so safe, now that you're in charge of the case." 

Lynn smiled prettily. 

The Captain asked sharply: "Didn't you before?" "Why, why, yes, of course. I didn't mean that the way it sounded, I suppose." "I hope you didn't, Mrs. Kennedy."

The conversation lapsed and after a pause of several minutes, Lynn inquired: "Well, what did happen to Gloria?" "I wouldn't be here asking, if I knew, Mrs. Kennedy." "Oh!"

Mrs. Curtis didn't appear to be quite the same assured young woman Captain Wyckoff had seen just the night before. Her hair was rumpled and she was wearing the same black dress. Wrinkles 70

and creases in the wrong places gave it an out-all-night-appearance which seemed to verify the reports of the detective about Mrs. Curtis.

Marian drew back a little when she saw the Captain at the door. 

Finally, she said, "Come in."

She closed the door and stood leaning against it with her hand on the doorknob. It almost looked as if she were determined to remain standing at the door in the hope that the Captain wouldn't stay long. "What is it, Captain?” she asked in a voice that was at once curt and gracious. 

The Captain answered pleasantly: "I wanted to talk over some new developments with you. May

A small black dog with white markings and a grave air thrust a nose out on the carpet in the Captain's direction from a vantage point near the fireplace and growled softly. "Hush!" Marian called. "Quiet, Susie." "That's all right," the Captain replied. "I like dogs. Here Susie," he called.

Distrustfully, Susie advanced cautiously toward the Captain's chair. The Captain reached down to pet her. "What kind is she?” "We never thought it would be tactful to ask her any questions like that," Marian responded with a weak smile. "Good idea. It might give her an inferiority complex. They're always smarter when they are a little mixed anyway." 71

APTAIN WYCKOFF found Gerry West in his office when he returned to Police Headquarters. "Hello, West. Have an idea, or is this a social call?" "Neither, Captain. I've been out working for you," the reporter answered airily. "Always glad to have assistance, West. Did you land something good?" "I don't know, Captain. I can't quite figure out what it means." The police car came to a halt before the Powers Hotel in West Forty-ninth Street, a half block from Seventh Avenue. A uniformed patrolman escorted them to a side entrance and they took an elevator to the twelfth floor.

A second policeman stood outside room No. 1204. When they entered Captain Wyckoff and the Sergeant found Dr. Himber, his assistant, and a house detective waiting for them. "Hello, Captain," Dr. Himber began. "I hear this is part of the Kennedy job?" "It might be," the Captain admitted with some hesitation. "Why don't you solve your murders before the urge to kill spreads all over town?" the Medical Examiner joshed. "I wish we could," the Captain replied seriously. "At least we know who the victim was this time. Let's get at it, Sergeant." "Nothing's been disturbed," Dr. Himber informed them. "She's in the bathroom,” he added, jerking a thumb toward a closed door off one side of the small hotel room. It was the usual tiny New 

York hotel room, with space for a single bed, a chest of drawers, a writing table, and a luggage rack at the foot of the bed. There was just room enough left for the five occupants to crowd in. 

Captain Wyckoff and the Sergeant stepped into the doorway of the bathroom. On the tile floor lay Helen Devere. At a glance the 

Captain saw why she had been a show girl for, although her face was distorted with pain, its youthful beauty was still almost haunting. "Another?" the Sergeant muttered involuntarily as he looked at the pain-twisted body. "Two beauts in one case. It don't average out, Captain. Usually we don't get one of these a year." "Suppose we drop this angle then," the Captain suggested sar- 79

castically. "If your hypothesis is correct, this suicide couldn't have had anything to do with the Kennedy case."

The Sergeant reddened under the rebuke, and the Captain continued to look carefully around the small bathroom. Its contents were strikingly neat except for the empty glass gripped tightly in the right hand of the dead woman. It had not been broken when she slumped to the floor and gave a crazily raucous touch to the still scene. "Jack House!" the Captain murmured, as if he were attempting to explain to himself a relationship that he could not comprehend, yet knew to exist, between the ex-convict and this beautiful dead girl. "And I thought he went in for the elderly type; most of those slickers do." "It's beyond me," the Sergeant averred. "A great many things are," Dr. Himber added irreverently. 

The detectives turned toward the Medical Examiner, and he pointed to a note lying on the writing table. The handwriting was bold and unequivocal, yet feminine. There were no evidences of uncertainty or mental aberration, such as are quite common in similar notes. Apparently Miss Devere had been a young woman with will power as well as beauty; at least, that is what the note indicated. 

Once determined upon suicide, she had proceeded to carry out her decision efficiently and expeditiously. Only the pain-snarled face gave any hint that, perhaps too late, she had had misgivings. The 

Captain leaned over the writing table and, without touching it or the note, read: "To whoever discovers my body: "I am writing this so that the circumstances of my death may be clear to anyone interested. There is no cause for alarm. . . ."

The Captain paused in amazement and stopped to reread the sentence: "There is no cause for alarm. There need be no suspicion of foul play. I have decided to end my life, and I am about to do it. "I realize that there will be many who will understand neither 80

this note nor my action. To these many, I have nothing to say. To a few others a very few, probably-who will try to understand, I say: I am tired and life is not as rosy as it seems. "Helen Devere (Kate Gillette). "P.S. I have no near relatives and only one for whom I care at all, although I sometimes wonder if that feeling is reciprocated. Perhaps 

Equity will undertake the matter of a funeral, if there must be one. 

I don't care myself. There are sufficient funds in my name at the 

Sixth National Bank to defray all expenses. I wish that Equity would use the balance to assist unemployed actors. "Hers?" the Captain inquired. "H.D." "The manager says that the handwriting is clearly Miss Devere's," the house detective answered. "Anything else, Himber?" "You have seen the whole show, Captain." "What poison did she use?" "My guess would be strychnine, but I can't tell until we have a chemical analysis made." "When did she die?" "Ask the Sergeant. He's your expert on that," Dr. Himber answered with a laugh. "Maybe this one will stump him." "He has already told me that it was about the time we had Jack 

House in for questioning-ten o'clock this morning." "It will take me all afternoon to get that close," the Medical

been trying for years to think of in connection with the Sergeant."
