Sergeant Kearney spoke a few emphatic words to the policeman on duty at the door and they started for the street again. They returned to Centre Street at a more leisurely pace than that at which they had made the trip uptown. The Captain was silent and the 

Sergeant, knowing from long experience that his superior was engaged in the process of working out the evidence in his mind, was quiet also.

Back in his office at Headquarters, the Captain galvanized into action, however. "Have House brought right in," he directed.

Jack House appeared promptly, no less dapper and insolent than he had been earlier in the day. He greeted the Captain jauntily: "How about assigning me a room down here, Captain? Why should I pay rent on an apartment?" "Just what I've been thinking," the Captain answered, like a flash. "We'll have a warrant waiting for you when we finish a little talk 

I plan to have with you right now." 82

"Warrant? What for?" House demanded, his face a picture of genuine surprise and fear. "You ought to know what warrants are for by now, House." "I mean why. What charge?" House asked quickly. "Detention as a material witness." 

House's fears left him. He argued loudly: "See here, Captain. You can't get away with that with me. I've got a solid alibi for all of yesterday. How many times do I have to explain that to you? I'll get my lawyer. "You can't hold me," he concluded defiantly. "We can't?" the Captain said calmly, as if House were objecting to self-evident, unalterable truths. "Why not?" "I know my rights. I'll demand an immediate hearing, and you aren't ready to take this case to court by a long shot," the confidence man explained. "This has nothing to do with the Kennedy case, House. We aren't asking your detention in that case at all." "What is the case then?" House exploded. "We're going to hold you in connection with the suicide of Miss 

Helen Devere." "What?" House cried. "The suicide of Helen Devere," the Captain repeated. "I don't know any plainer or shorter words, and you can't tell me that you don't know her." "Sure, I know her, Captain. I told you so yesterday. But what's this suicide business? Another one of your lousy police tricks, I suppose." The Captain didn't reply, and House added: "She was feeling fine only last night." "So I understand," the Captain answered grimly. 

Suddenly, House turned almost white. "Tell me what's happened," he begged. 83

"That," the Captain said firmly, "is just what we were expecting you to do for us. You tell us about it."

House seemed to cringe as the Captain turned his icy steel eyes on him. The whitish cast appeared to dance as the Captain stared the confidence man down. “I nev—never heard of it until this minute," House stammered. "She wasn't that kind. She had everything to live for, too." "She apparently didn't think so," the Captain said, adding significantly, "if it was suicide."

Captain Wyckoff's eyes continued to bore into House, as the exconvict insisted: "She was perfectly all right at dinner last night. When did she do it? How and where?" he pleaded. Tears dribbled down his cheeks.

Captain Wyckoff explained briefly, and House broke down completely, sobbing uncontrollably. "I loved that girl," he wailed. "I never would have harmed a hair of her head. I would have died for her in a minute. Why didn't she tell me what was wrong!"

The Captain sat unmoved. "I suppose you told much the same story to most of those emotionstarved widows you robbed," he remarked. "Captain Wyckoff," House begged through tear-blurred eyes, "for once in my life I'm on the level. I meant every word of that." "Unfortunately, House, your life-long record is against you.

Besides, I understand that you are a great talker-a very fine persuader. Your record proves it." "Not this time," House argued.

The Captain leaned forward, looked straight into the eyes of the confidence man, and pointed a long finger at him: "Stop blubbering! This is exactly the kind of a murder that you would commit, House. It is diabolical enough to be just your game and you know it.” 84

"Murder, Captain? You said it was suicide!" "That, House, may explain just why there are no murders in long police record. You get away with it as suicide." "No!" House screamed. your "Oh, we'll oblige you, House, and call it suicide for a whileofficially-so long as you hoped we'd think that." "You're dead wrong this time."

The Captain continued to press for an admission: "Does a beautiful young woman with everything to live for, a job, money, friends, happiness—you admit that yourself-suddenly decide to take poison for no reason at all?” 

House remained silent. "Does she?" the Captain prodded. "Or is it because someone persuades her to kill herself-someone, like yourself, who talks nicely?" "Don't be absurd, Captain." "It's been done, House. You yourself have handled more difficult jobs of persuading. Lots of women part with money more reluctantly than they do with their lives-up until that last second when the realization dawns on them that they're really going to lose their lives." 

House burst out crying again. 

Impatiently, the Captain commanded: "Turn off the faucets, House. Tears don't move me the way they may certain ladies of your acquaintance. Now, why did you do it?" "I didn't," House insisted tearfully. "I'm on the level in this."

The Captain beckoned to Sergeant Kearney, who had been sitting in a far corner of the office. "Sergeant, House is wasting our time. Have the boys downstairs talk to him for a while and perhaps he'll be glad to see us again later."

The Sergeant ambled forward with set lips: "We'll erl up his tonsils for his tonsils for you a bit.” 85

"Captain, please!" House pleaded.

Memories of scores of other sessions with broad-backed detectives, who "talked" with lengths of rubber hose on the abdomen and kidneys as their most cogent arguments were too strong. "Not that, please! I'm trying my best to answer your questions." "You aren't telling me what I want to hear," the Captain insisted. "I can't. I didn't do it, I swear." "I'll give you one more chance. Now, House, what sort of psychic nonsense did you spray all over Helen Devere and why was it so effective?" "I did nothing of the sort, Captain. Honest!" "What did you two talk about at dinner last night?" "Not suicide, Captain." "I'm certain that you didn't," the Captain commented with disgust. "You're much too subtle for that." "You're wrong, Captain." "House, it's quite useless to talk to you. You have lived by your wits so long and been in and out of so many police stations that no one could ever persuade you to tell a small fraction of the truth. I didn't expect to get anything this time. I want you to know, however, that I hold you responsible-directly responsible for the death of Helen Devere, however you accomplished it. I suppose the best guess is some sort of blackmail. You knew something about her and threatened her with it last night. Didn't you?" 

Again those searing eyes blazed full at House. "No," he managed to insist. "Why, I don't know-yet," the Captain went on, as if thinking out loud. believe me, Captain. Unfortunately, as you remarked, I haven't a reputation for veracity. For once, I wish that I had. I'd like to be able to convince you that Helen meant more to me than anything in the world." "What a moving appeal!" the Captain commented caustically. "We'll know soon. All right, House, you can go now.' "But, Captain," Sergeant Kearney objected. "What about this warrant?" he said, slapping his hip pocket. "Don't lose it, Sergeant. Right now this microbe would have a lawyer in here to spring him in about twenty minutes." "The boys can say a lot in twenty minutes," he urged. "No, I guess not, Sergeant. Watch your step, House. Next time, we'll make it stick." "You'll never get me on Helen's suicide, Captain," House repeated as he backed out of the office. The Sergeant pursued him, with hand upraised as though about to strike him. "Leave this egg out, Eddie," the Sergeant bawled. "Beat it!" he added to House, giving him a shove through the doorway. 

The Sergeant returned to the desk. "How did it strike you?" the Captain asked. "He's a lyin', of course," the Sergeant replied firmly. "He's a smart egg though. He knows the courts don't go that physical stuff." "That what, Sergeant?" "That physical stuff you said he fed the gal." 

The Captain roared. "We must make more of an effort to keep things down to your level, Mike." 

The Sergeant looked puzzled: "Captain, I was just gettin' a laugh. Anyway, he knows we don't know what he told the dame." "I'm not so certain he was lying, Mike." "Aw, you should've let the boys bounce him a bit. He would've broke. He ain't so tough." 87

"Are you certain?" "Tough guys don't blubber like babies," the Sergeant said, citing what was for him uncontestable evidence to prove his point. "Listen, Mike. We don't know a thing about this case yet. We may be biting just the way whoever did it wants us to." "Well, at least we ain't being obligin' on purpose, Captain." "I hope not, Mike. "Then, there's always the chance that, although the Kennedy murderer did this job also, House was not in on the first murder.” "Just a chance, Captain. I ain't so naa-ive, however. . . ."

Although apparently inclined to continue on, Sergeant Kearney stopped for he noticed that his superior had slipped forward, rested his head on his folded arms and gone to sleep. "Damn it," the Sergeant muttered to himself. "He never will listen to my angles. He always goes to sleep on me."

The Sergeant sighed sadly, dropped his huge body into a leather easy chair and sat down to await developments, confident either that the Captain would awaken in a few minutes or that something would happen to rouse him.

The latter happened, for within a few minutes the telephone rang. 

Automatically, the Captain reached for it. 

Dr. Himber was ready to make a preliminary report on the 

Devere suicide. "We're waiting," the Captain answered. 

The Medical Examiner appeared promptly. "Fast work this time, Doc," the Captain commented. "Beginning to take an interest in your work, or does beauty always affect you that way?" "I'll admit it's a relief to come across one that isn't all mildewed with age, Captain." "What's the story, Doc?" 88

"It was strychnine, Captain. She took enough to kill three horses in about as many minutes. She died quickly, naturally, but the size of the dose she took would explain the tortured expression on her face." "Taken in water?" "Seems so, Captain, and before breakfast, too, if you must have all the horrible details. For myself, I'll stick to orange juice as an eye opener.' 

"Mike likes prunes," the Captain reported. "However, we're supposed to be working on a murder case, so let's forget breakfast for a while. Any chance that the poison was slipped to Miss Devere as bicarbonate of soda or something like that?" "Hardly." I'm taking it for granted that she put it there herself." "Women are funny, Captain, but not that crazy usually." "Suppose there was a possibility of blackmail, or some other good reason." "That's different. The Sergeant's men ought to be able to answer that. They should have her room torn to small pieces by now. The Sergeant returned almost at once with Pete Handley, a plain-clothes man. "Pete just came in, Captain. He can give us the cold dope. Get it off your chest, Pete," he directed the detective. Reports on where 

House was at the moment of writing and what he was doing in a social way. No mention of his work, though," Pete added with a grin. "Affectionate?" "About the way you would write your Aunt Hattie, if you have one."

to get along in the world. She studied all day in addition to her stage work. She had hopes of becoming an opera singer. She was studying Italian and dramatic art in addition to voice." CHAPTER 

5 AR BOUT the same time, Lynn Kennedy sat alone at a corner table in the Cafe des Boulevards fidgeting over a scotch and soda. It was her third and between sips she peered nervously toward the circular door that led out to Broadway. An ash tray, half filled with cigaret stubs, rested on the table before her. She glanced at her wrist watch for the twentieth time and hurriedly lighted a fresh cigaret from the one she was finishing. It was the quiet hour before cocktail time and the cafe was empty except for a few loungers at the bar. 

Lynn looked at her watch once more and craned her neck for a last glance at the street door. She raised her hand and crooked a finger toward the waiter. Then, she waved him away as the circular door began to turn and she saw Gerry West entering. Without a glance to either side of him, he walked directly toward her table, as if by habit or appointment. "Darling, I've been terrified that you wouldn't come," Lynn greeted him, her face glowing. "I had a hell of a time getting here. The City Editor has been pumping me to see if I didn't know something that I'm not giving him." 

Gerry glanced toward the door, before continuing: "We really shouldn't meet until this mess is cleared up. We've 94

probably been followed by the Sergeant's men-to say nothing of my fellow reporters-the rats. I wouldn't be surprised if my own sheet had a tail on me.” "The Sergeant's a goof," Lynn replied. "Sure," Gerry agreed, "but he's a good cop. He's harder to lose than hay fever." "I know," Lynn said, touching his sleeve fondly, "but I don't trust the telephone either, and I had to see you to tell you something." "Yes?" "I think Martin suspects something. We had an awful row last night. He was as mysterious as only Martin can be about something-a lot of things. I'm afraid he either knows something or thinks he does anyway-which is worse." 

Gerry whistled through his teeth. "God," he replied, "I suppose we were fools to think we could get away with it. This is a fine mess, if you're guessing right. The best thing for us to do is not to see each other or to even phone, and we'd better clear out right now. You leave first-and don't ever forget that you were at the movies yesterday afternoon. Don't try to get in touch with me unless something breaks and you have to. I'll come around only when Martin is home-and if you have anything to tell me you can slip me a note. "Be nice to Martin and turn on the old appeal. I hate to think of your having to do that, but it's the only way: make him forget, if he knows; and change his mind, if he's only guessing." 

Lynn stood up. "Sometime it will be different for us, if we ever get out of this. 

I'll do all you say, Gerry, but you know it won't be easy." 

Her eyes softened and she bent over and kissed him swiftly.

Then she hurried through the door. Gerry suspected that she was on the verge of tears. 95

Martin and Lynn sat on opposite sides of the fireplace in their livingroom. Martin was holding the folded Evening Star in front of him but he wasn't reading. Lynn, curled up like a contented cat in her chair, was actually far from contented. Her eyes were dark and troubled. Martin was not reacting as he should and always had when she bothered to turn on her charm for him. Tonight he seemed not to notice. "That picture of me the cameraman snapped as I went through the door is too ghastly, isn't it?" Lynn asked, wrinkling her nose up and laughing lightly.

Martin started, as if he'd been a long way off when her voice pulled him back. "What?" he asked. "Oh, yes-the picture. Blair always takes a good picture."

Lynn laughed again, but there was more than a little edge to her voice, as she said, "You weren't listening, I meant mine. Mine was vile, but hers was worse. Those feet! But, of course, it's too realistic and I don't suppose a cameraman would bother to make her feet look less like those of that Sergeant who keeps creeping around here. Her face is a bit horsey, but if you care for that rugged type of beauty "Oh," she said, swinging to her feet, "I'm being catty, but my nerves are on edge-being watched every second and remembering that horrid thing there in the closet, I can't sleep." 

Her voice broke into a sob and her shoulders quivered. "Martin, take me away."

She sank down on Martin's lap and buried her head on his shoulder. Martin put his arms around her, but his eyes looked straight ahead and there was no pity in them for her. "You know," he said, "that they wouldn't let us get as far as

Grand Central. We've got to stay and face it. It's not easy for me either." 

Lynn's head came up quickly. For Martin to consider himself 96

and his feelings when she felt so miserable was certainly not like him, and utterly selfish. Then, she realized how strangely Martin was acting. Promptly, she forgot her promise to Gerry, as her temper, held too long in check, lashed out at Martin. Martin was saved a torrent of accusations only by the fact that just then the apartment bell rang. "Damn," said Lynn, "now who could that be?"

She smoothed her hair and rearranged her makeup. Gone was the discontented, distorted ugliness. Instantly, she looked like a very weary and lovely child. Martin marveled at her ability as an actress, and sighed a little as he went to answer the bell. "Returning to the scene of the crime?" he asked, admitting Marian. "Don't joke," Marian pleaded. "I'm jittery enough already. I've never put in such a twenty-four hours in my life." "Me, too," Lynn agreed. "Martin and I were just discussing how beastly it is. It's spoiling our fun. I usually enjoy our evenings together so." "I don't wonder," Marian said, "I've been dying of lonesomeness all day. Thank goodness, George will be home in the morning." "So soon?" Martin inquired. "Didn't he intend to stay longer than one day?" "Well, I don't really know," Marian replied uncertainly. "I suppose he's worrying about this mess." "It will be comforting to have him back,” Lynn said. “George is so reliable in difficult situations." "It's his business training, I suppose," Marian agreed. “Anyway, I'll be grateful. "I've been dying to talk to someone about it all day long." 97

No one had another suggestion to make. "We haven't learned much," Gerry resumed, "but perhaps we've 102

Martin stepped to the house telephone and talked briefly with 

Dr. Ross and Mr. Eubank. Jack House did not answer.

Almost at once Mr. and Mrs. Eubank and Dr. Ross rang the doorbell. 

Mrs. Eubank again wore her canary-yellow robe over pajamas of the same shade and carried a moist handkerchief in her hand. "I shouldn't have come over this way, but I'm just busting with curiosity," she explained. "Besides, Mr. Kennedy sounded so hopeless over the phone, Taylor said." 103

"Oh, he always sounds that way," Blair explained. "It's just marriage. Really, he's more human than he sounds most of the time." "I'll explain about Martin, if you don't mind," Lynn said icily. "Of course," Blair agreed affably, "if you feel that you must.

There was a positiveness about Dr. Ross that encouraged both confidence and respect. Her stature and the fiber of her deep voice commanded attention. Plainly, she was a pillar of strength for her patients, and a dominating personality, but her quiet manner belied that to some extent. "Of course," Martin murmured. "Good evening. This is . . .”

Dr. Ross interrupted: 

"Never mind, I just heard it all."

"By that time, I had worked myself into quite a lather," 

Mrs. Eubank added, with a rippling laugh. "I imagine I was just as excited as Magnolia by the time she reached our apartment. "Well, I've never seen such a frightened-looking Negro in all my life. She was sort of grayish-looking, and I suppose that's as near to white as a good scare can make any colored person. Of course, I had surmised that the murder here had a lot to do with Magnolia's being so frightened. I imagined that she might have felt that the lightning was a bit too close for comfort and had decided to forsake these parts for more comfortable ones. You know how darkies are! It was too obvious. “At that time I hadn't seen an afternoon paper and I didn't know that Gloria had disappeared over night, or it would have been so much easier to deal with Magnolia. However, in the end, she weakened. It began as it usually does, when Negroes get into trouble, by her asking if I would take care of her, if she got into a mess. theoretically at least shouldn't have been there, waiting impatiently for the elevator. "This was just a little before four o'clock as near as Magnolia could remember. She went back to her own room, and apparently just stayed there too scared to move out until I reached her on the telephone. "That's all I could get out of her," Mrs. Eubank concluded. The two police officers left a bewildered group behind them.

"Do what, Captain?" "Just ease your cerebral tenseness." "Never mind, Captain. I'm sorry I brought it up," the Sergeant answered, with a bewildered shake of his head. "Your explanations only mix me up more."

While the Captain and Sergeant were returning to Headquarters, a glum group of five continued a morose and pointless conversation in apartment 8B, merely because the Kennedys' three friends were reluctant to go home and no one felt in the mood for sleep anyway. "I wish we could be of some value to the police," Martin was saying for the seventh time. "There should be some way in which intelligent citizens could assist at a time like this instead of sitting around and lamenting their lot." "We tried, didn't we?" Gerry asked in exasperation. "And where did we get?" "And Dr. Ross who knows all about mental processes suggested that we should try again," Blair added. "Too late," Gerry moaned. "I know how these things work. In just about another twenty-four hours the Captain will be putting the heavy hand on all of us." "Why?" Lynn asked with surprise. "By that time, Lynn, the police will have exhausted all the routine approaches to the identification of this man. It doesn't look as if they were going to be very successful. That will leave the police just about where they started last night-plus a suicide and the disappearance of your maid-why did that fool gal have to run?-so they'll start in on us, and will be really tough, for the 

Captain will be convinced that some one of us ought to know this man." 

Lynn went into a violent coughing spell. Marian dashed to her side and pounded her on the back. 114

Martin, who sat nearby, thought that he heard Marian whisper, "Careful, now, Lynn," but he couldn't have been certain about that. 

More sobered than before by Gerry's prediction of what lay before them, the group broke up in silence a few minutes later. 

As Blair and Gerry walked westward toward the subway, Blair asked suddenly: "Do you suppose that bridge invitation was just a blind, so we'd be there when the body turned up?” "Don't be stupid," Gerry advised. "I suppose you're going to remind me that the Kennedys are our friends," his companion continued. "It wouldn't hurt to remember it at a time like this." "I'm glad I called her on that when we were playing truth, nevertheless." "Sure, sure. You got Lynn all upset for nothing." "Do you really think I did!" Blair responded with a gay laugh. 115

CHAPTER 

WHEN the Chicago Express pulled into the Grand Central 

Station the following morning, a detective was standing on the station platform reading the Pullman car numbers as they coasted past him. He picked out car No. 937 and ran alongside the door until the train stopped. As the porter opened the door to carry out the baggage, he flashed a shield in the porter's awe-filled eyes and said, "Lower nine?" "Here's his bag, suh," the porter answered, placing it carefully on the platform. The detective stood beside the bag until George 

Curtis stepped from the train and reached for it. The detective tapped him on the arm. "I'm from Headquarters, Mr. Curtis," he announced, showing his shield again. "Captain Wyckoff of the Homicide Squad wants to talk with you. He asked me to drive you right downtown."

George Curtis displayed no great amount of surprise, and inquired calmly: "Why, yes. What's it all about?" "You must have read about the murder in the apartment of the 

Kennedys-friends of yours." "Naturally." "That's it," the detective answered briefly. 116

Mr. Curtis waited for a further explanation, which was not forthcoming, before inquiring: "What has that to do with rushing me downtown?" "The Captain thought you might be helpful," was the detective's noncommittal response. "Of course, I'll be glad to assist in any way that I can-for the sake of the Kennedys," Mr. Curtis said, "but I do think this is a trifle inconsiderate of your Captain." "Mister, he decides about that," the detective answered bluffly. "All right, I'm ready any time you are," George agreed.

The detective escorted George to a squad car parked outside the Vanderbilt Avenue entrance to the station and they drove quickly downtown. George found the Captain sitting at his desk with the 

Sergeant alongside going through a sheaf of reports.

They both looked dissatisfied. 

Captain Wyckoff greeted him cordially: "Sorry to have to give you the impression that we had what the newspapers like to call 'the drag net' out for you, Mr. Curtis. That isn't true, of course, but we do need any help that you can give us. "We can't solve a crime,” he explained pleasantly, "until we know who was killed, and we are stuck proper this time. Would you take a look at this photograph, please?"

He handed over a photograph of the murdered man, and George studied it with care. His hands shook slightly, but the 

Captain could not tell whether it was emotion or a cigaret palsy that caused the picture to quiver in George's hand. George continued to examine the photograph slowly, thoughtfully chewing his under lip. At last, he answered, "I don't know this man, Captain." “Hm-mmm,” the Captain hesitated. "You're quite certain?” "Absolutely." "Never saw him around the apartment house in which you live?" "No." 117

"How about this fellow then?" the Captain inquired, handing him a picture of Jackson Hatteras. 

Almost immediately, George answered: "This is the confidence man, isn't it? I saw his picture in the morning newspapers." "That's right. Know him?” "Well, I certainly don't know him. There's something a little familiar about his face. I may have seen him in the elevator, or perhaps it's just because I saw his picture in the papers." "Didn't you see the other man in the papers also?" "Yes, but somehow, it didn't register like this one. You know how it is, Captain. Sometimes our imagination plays tricks on us, and we begin to believe that we know someone when we don't. 

It's like that with this photograph." "Did you think of that when you first saw House's picture on the front pages this morning?" "Well, no, I didn't, but I wasn't paying much attention then. Of course," he said with a laugh, "the answer is obvious: I've seen 

House in the elevator." "I was wondering if you ever bumped into him in San Francisco?" the Captain inquired suddenly.

George appeared puzzled for a few seconds. He hesitated, then replied: "Did I know him out on the Coast?" "That's the question, Mr. Curtis." "No, I'm certain that I didn't." "This being New York," the Captain said good-naturedly, "it couldn't have been the San Francisco fog that played tricks on your imagination." "A typical Eastern gag," George answered indulgently. "Well, we're in one on this case," the Captain complained with a helpless shrug of his shoulders. "Can't get anywhere. Nothing 118

works out right. Here, I was hoping that you could give us a hand with the identification, and you're just about our last hope. That's a detective's life for you."

Captain Wyckoff sank back in his chair and thoughtfully shook his head over the woeful lot of a detective. "Say," he shot out quickly, "what did you go to Chicago for anyway?" "Business," George replied quietly. "Travel much?" "Seldom. I usually let the younger lads do it, but occasionally 

I have to make a trip. I couldn't get out of this one." "Cleaned it up quickly, didn't you?" the Captain suggested. "Yes. Affairs didn't turn out exactly as I expected." "So, you went to the movies," Captain Wyckoff added rapidly. 

George Curtis drew his breath in slowly. "So, you've been checking up on me," he said without rancor. "I thought that you would, when I read the stories in the newspapers. 

"Naturally," the Captain replied. "Now, would you care to change your mind about identifying either one or both of those photographs?" "If a business trip fails to turn out as expected,” George answered heatedly, "does it necessarily follow that I'm a liar?” "No, not necessarily." "Let's assume that I'm not then." "I'll be glad to assume that, Mr. Curtis, if you'll explain why within an hour or so, maybe less, after a murder was committed in the apartment of friends of yours and in which your wife has admitted being at almost the same time as when a man was killed there, you decided suddenly that business called and hopped out for Chicago?" "Gladly, Captain. It was a trip of a highly confidential nature. 119

The necessity for it arose without advance warning, and it was a matter which was difficult to entrust to one of my subordinates." "Exactly, Mr. Curtis, but would you care to be a little more explicit?" "Frankly, Captain, it isn't a matter that concerns you." "Perhaps not, but you ought to let us decide that for ourselves." "I don't see that my business affairs are a matter for police investigation," George insisted. "In the circumstances they are." "All right, Captain. I won't argue the point. There's no secret about where I went." "Not now," the Captain remarked. "I don't like your way of saying that, Captain." "If you can explain where you went satisfactorily, I'll be only too glad to apologize, Mr. Curtis." "I had to obtain reports on a confidential investigation concerning a client of my firm-there's a substantial sum of money at stake." "What's the name of your client, Mr. Curtis?" "I don't care to say-unless it's absolutely necessary for the purposes of your investigation." "It is," the Captain answered firmly. "Wouldn't you be willing to accept instead the sworn statement of my partners with respect to the accuracy of what I have told you?" "That would be all right, Mr. Curtis-if you can get it," the 

Captain responded with a grin. "What do you mean, if I can get it'? I'll have it for afternoon," George promised. you this "Are you certain of that?" the Captain asked. "One of your partners seemed a bit upset to learn yesterday that you were in Chicago." 120

"What's that?" George demanded. "Mr. Adams seemed a little confused about your movements, Mr. Curtis. Was there any particular reason why he shouldn't have been informed about this very confidential mission of yours, or are the firm's affairs no concern of his either?"

George's hands quivered as he drummed nervously on the desk with his fingertips. He answered very cautiously: "I know that it sounds like a falsehood, Captain, but it happens that Mr. Adams, alone of our three senior partners, wasn't informed of the reasons for my trip to Chicago." "Why not?" "Let me finish, please.' 

The Captain nodded and George continued: "The others were. How Mr. Adams reached the conclusion that 

I was in Washington on private affairs is a question which I can't answer without consulting him first." "How's that?" the Captain asked. "I don't recall that Mr.

Adams said anything about Washington-or private affairs either." "He didn't . . . ?” ...

George bit his lip and his fingers drummed nervously on the desk. "It must'a been a couple of other boids," the Sergeant commented. "You aren't trying to fool Mr. Adams, too, are you?" the Captain asked with a chuckle. "How dare you suggest such a thing," George demanded, his heavy face more flushed than ever. "No suggestion from this quarter," the Captain answered calmly. "It's an idea you're giving me. Want to talk some more now?" "No, I do not," George said indignantly. "There's been a mistake somewhere, but not of my making. A decent citizen has a right to go to Chicago whenever he desires without having to explain all his 121

affairs to the police. If a murder happens to be committed about the same time he departs, that's not complete proof that he's a fugitive from justice." "Of course, not, Mr. Curtis, but you can see our position, surely?" "All I know is that you have butted into my business affairs without reason or tact. Apparently you trailed me all over Chicago yesterday and then you rushed a man up to nab me the minute I stepped off a train this morning. I might ask: what do you plan next, Captain?"

George Curtis spoke with a defiant air that his shaking hands and flushed face didn't quite make convincing. "I thought that you'd suggest the next move," the Captain responded nonchalantly. "Well, I won't," George said firmly. "You don't want to be more explicit in your explanation of the very apparent incongruities—to use a polite word-in your story?" "I do not, Captain, and what's more I've said all that I intend to say." "And more," the Sergeant amended. "Too bad," the Captain remarked evenly. "I guess that we'll have to get Mr. Adams to straighten out the yarn." "I suggest that you don't try," George answered with a snap of his jaw. "Take it easy, Mr. Curtis. We're only trying to get at the facts. 

You ought to be glad to help us." 

George Curtis didn't reply. Instead, he turned and stamped out of the office. Captain Wyckoff made no effort to stop him. 

After the door slammed violently, the Sergeant looked up with a broad Irish grin on his face. "A queer duck," he remarked. it "He is a trifle touchy," the Captain agreed. "Still, we do go a wee bit strong sometimes. We should learn to be more tactful 122

with successful business men particularly, Mike. They aren't accustomed to being interrogated; they give orders." "His story would be okay, Captain, if he could remember what city he was supposed to be in yesterday, or if one of his boys would tell him." "Business men have so much on their minds," the Captain replied breezily. "Let's get back to that shoe report. You were just about to amaze me with it when Mr. Curtis dropped in." "Here it is," the Sergeant said, sifting a sheet of letterheadsized paper out of the mass of reports before him on the desk. "I'll read it for you." "All right, but just the conclusions," the Captain commanded. “I know how you love to read out loud." 

Sergeant Kearney cleared his throat noisily and began to read: "From this, I conclude that these soles were prepared in a somewhat crude manner. Coitainly this leather was never sold as prime

"Yes? How so?" "Well, Captain, to begin at the beginning: Sergeant Kearney detailed me to stay at his heels until relieved. So I picked him up when he left your office. He stays in his office about fifteen minutes and suddenly he races out, slamming the door. He's in a hell of a hurry, and I don't expect him so he almost knocks me down. Captain Wyckoff was absorbed in thought on the ride uptown, but the Sergeant was inclined to be talkative. "Do you figure he left somethin' around he don't want us to find?" he inquired.

The Captain's firm “No” reduced the Sergeant to silence for a few blocks, but his curiosity overcame him again shortly: "Maybe him and that Kennedy skoit ain't on th' level," was his next suggestion. "Nonsense, Mike."

The Sergeant thought that over for a few minutes, and then announced triumphantly: 133

"I got it, Captain. That Kennedy dame knows th' angle, and he's puttin' th' gag on her."

The Captain lifted a restraining hand. "You babble on like an old maid, Mike. Why not wait and see?"

The Captain was free to think without interruption during the remainder of the trip.

As they left the elevator at the eighth floor, a uniformed policeman joined them. "He's left-only stayed about fifteeen minutes. Quigley's with him." "Where?" "His own place, I guess. At least, he went up to the seventeenth floor." "We'll go in anyway," the Captain decided.

The doorbell rang several times before Lynn answered it. 

There was clear evidence that her nose had been hastily daubed with powder while they waited, for red spots showed through. Nor were the inadequate daubs any concealment of Lynn's bloodshot eyes or the fact that she had been crying very recently. "You-again?" she stammered when she saw who her callers were. "Yes, we're here again," the Captain responded. "We don't seem to be very welcome." "Why, of course, you are," Lynn added hurriedly. "It was just that I didn't expect to see you again so soon." "Do ya think we was goin' to lay down on this case?" the Sergeant demanded in his most officious tone. "The Captain and me don't operate that way." "I can believe it," Lynn answered coldly. "The Sergeant has his conscience," the Captain explained mildly. "Most of us do," Lynn answered without interest. "The Sergeant's works," the Captain said, again with that sharp glint in his steely eyes. 134

"I understand that's a rebuke," Lynn replied calmly. "What for? Has something new happened?" "No," the Captain said thoughtfully. "This case has so many unusual angles. We're unearthing more of them all the time. I suppose-in fact, I know-that there are others which could be brought to light with the proper cooperation on the part of some of you people." "Yes?" "That's all, Mrs. Kennedy. This isn't a lecture hall. You people all have the idea that you can best defend yourselves by trying to cover up facts and telling us untruths." "I can't speak for anyone else, Captain," Lynn shouted, "but I certainly haven't told you any lies." "Probably not, Mrs. Kennedy, but on the other hand I don't believe that you've been too scrupulous about giving us all the facts at your command." "I've told you everything that I know, Captain. Further, I certainly resent the implication that I haven't." "I'll take you at your word, Mrs. Kennedy, with regard to the past," the detective said carelessly, but those keen eyes captured and held Lynn's. "Why did George Curtis come here to see you a few minutes ago?"

Almost hypnotized, as her husband had been before and many others before him, by the icy inquisition of the Captain's eyes, Lynn said nothing for a minute. "It was a social call," she answered. "A social call? At this time of morning." "Yes. He dropped in to express his regret over the terrible publicity we have been subjected to by this miserable business." "That's all?" "Yes," Lynn answered so softly that the Captain barely heard her. 

Again those hard eyes bored into Lynn's. 135

"He didn't act like a man making a sympathy call," the Captain said evenly. 

Lynn did not reply. The Captain waited for a few minutes, then turned abruptly on his heel and limped toward the door. "Back to Headquarters, Sergeant," he commanded.

As they entered the Homicide Squad Headquarters sometime later, the man at the desk said tersely: "Folsom's asking for his picture, Captain." 136

CHAPTER 

7 

HE response came in promptly from Folsom Prison. 

The murdered man was Wilbur Moore, aged forty-three years, formerly of San Francisco, sentenced from there in 

1930 for grand larceny, a perfect prisoner, released on parole for good behavior ten days before his death. He had had one earlier conviction against his record, in 1914, for a minor robbery and had served two years for that offense. There were no known relatives. 

Captain Wyckoff smiled expansively as he looked over the brief but satisfying report from the prison authorities. "No question about this, of course?" he asked Sergeant Kearney. "They got th' pitchers," the Sergeant replied. "I guess we can take their woid for it. Besides, Hoover's boys just come through with th' same dope. Pitchers and prints check all 'round." "Good. My congratulations to Dr. Himber and your hide expert; they knew what they were talking about all the time. We progress, Mike, and I can't honestly say that I regard Mr. Moore's sad end as any great loss to society." "Some bloke prob'bly did the woild a good toin, but our job is to run him down and give 'im the woiks," the Sergeant said dourly. "So we must, Mike. We're back in the California whirl again. 

This just about makes it unanimous." 137

"House did a toin in Folsom in '30," the Sergeant reminded his chief, with a return of his old enthusiasm for the chase. "And the Curtises were in San Francisco," his superior added. "So was Mrs. Kennedy," the Sergeant enlarged pointedly. "I guess we'll have to see them all again, Mike. Suppose we start with House and Curtis, in that order, assuming, of course, that 

House isn't too grief-stricken to come downtown." "And if he is?" the Sergeant asked with a laugh. "Bring him down anyway. The exercise will do him good." "I'll get the boys started at once, Captain. I got one to spring on 'im, too," the Sergeant answered, starting through the office door. 

He ducked back quickly to add: "A whole regiment of Richard Harding Davises is outside. Do you want that Moore angle spilled?" "Why not, Mike? Send them in; I'll talk to them."

The Sergeant walked back to the door and opened it. A dozen police reporters plowed past him and converged at the Captain's desk. "What's the break, Cap?" the Journal man inquired while still in full stride toward the desk. "Is there a new development?" the Times man inquired politely, while the Mirror representative demanded: "Spring it, Cap!" "We probably printed it yesterday," he added confidently. "I don't doubt that you did," the Captain answered affably, “but, for the benefit of the papers without Delphic Oracles, I wanted to announce that we have identified the man who was found murdered in the Kennedy apartment." "Why the hell didn't you say so?" several reporters shouted almost in unison. 

Captain Wyckoff quickly explained the Moore identification and how it had been established. 138

"Any questions?" he concluded. "Yeah," the Journal man replied at once. "What are you going to do about this Jack House now?" "He's being brought in for questioning again." "All your gang ever does is to bring people in for questioning, Cap," the News man lamented. "That doesn't make headlines." "I hadn't noticed that the News was appearing without them lately," the Captain responded quietly. "You know what I mean," the News reporter said. “Let's have an arrest, just to vary the dull routine." "There's a law against false arrest," the detective went on informatively. "Come, come, Cap," the Mirror man said, joining in the discussion. "Don't act so young. Your boys only guess right on about one pinch in four." "Well," the Captain said, getting up to end the interview, “if any one of you can drag a confession out of anyone connected with this case now, we'll be glad to oblige with an arrest. We can't even find anyone who knows Moore." "Old stuff, too thin," the Journal man commented. “Murder mystery baffles police." "Never mind spelling out the words, Butch," the Mirror reporter broke in. "I have an idea, Cap. How about letting us talk to that House fellow when you get through with him?” "Has anyone stopped you from talking to House to your heart's content?" the Captain asked, bristling. "This department's not run that way and you boys know it as well as I do." "Sure, Cap, sure. No offense intended. I'll admit there aren't quite as many stuffed shirts on this run as there are over at the courthouse, for instance. You know what we mean: this House fellow won't stay parked long enough for us to whip out a pencil on him." 139

"So you want me to make a flying tackle and sit on his chest while you boys drag his right name out of him?" the Captain inquired smoothly. "You put it stiffly," the Mirror representative agreed, "but that's the general idea." "Sorry, lads. I'd do anything in my power for you, as you should know, but I can't arrest a man, who is still innocent of any crime so far as we know, so you can ply him with questions while he's under lock and key." "Come off it, Cap," the Mirror reporter pleaded. "There isn't any municipal election this fall. Besides, you don't have to feed us that civic righteousness stuff. That's why I got myself taken off the City Hall beat. It got so I was lying even when it wasn't necessary, just from listening to those 'have-a-cigar-oh, take-two' boys operate." "Your city editor ought to supply you with a nursemaid," the 

Captain remarked, with a smile. "No, Cap," the Mirror man argued seriously. "This House is a hard case, as you should know by now. He's had too much experience with detectives and reporters. Why, Hauptmann was loquacious by comparison. House doesn't talk." "So we learned," the Captain remarked tersely. "I thought it was just us up to now, but I'm glad to learn that other people have trouble, too. Rest assured, if we find cause to put him away, you'll have full visiting privileges.' 

"Thanks, Cap, that's all we wanted."

"The boys were just complaining that he wasn't talkative enough to suit them.” "He's babbling, Captain-babbling plenty, right now, but it's all about th' injustice of cops. The Sergeant had spoken the complete truth about House's state of mind. He was neither jaunty, as he had been on his first visit, nor dejected, as he had been the second time, but boiling with anger. He burst into the room and addressed the Captain vindictively: "Ask all your questions this time, Captain, because this is my last voluntary trip down here." "Oh, we decide when you're to make them, House; you could hardly call them entirely voluntary," the Captain answered. "The next time, Captain, you'll need that warrant you were talking about so freely." "Well, we'll have it, if you insist upon it." "And I'll have my lawyer ready, too," the confidence man shouted. "You'll need the woim," the Sergeant threatened. "Maybe sooner than you expect, putty face." "Call your bloodhound off, Captain," House said, crooking a savage finger at the Sergeant. "He should know by this time that

I don't scare like most of the punks he has to deal with.” "Maybe you will this time, wise guy," the Sergeant thundered. "There's always a foist time with you hard mutts." "I doubt that," House replied, equally pugnaciously. "I haven't any way of knowing what it takes to frighten you into 141

telling a straight story," the Captain intervened, "but, House, I wasn't joking about that warrant.” "Yes?" the confidence man retorted. "Serve it then; I dare you." "Perhaps we will," the Captain answered quietly, "but, first, I think I'll tell you that we've identified the man who was found murdered in the Kennedy apartment." "His relatives will be pleased, no doubt," House said, without any apparent interest in the matter. "He was a friend of yours," the Captain continued, speaking deliberately and studying House's facial expressions closely, but probably without real hope that any sincere display of emotion would be reflected there. "So!" House answered. "Funny I didn't recognize him myself in that case. Maybe Old Brass Toes here,” he resumed, calmly pointing at the Sergeant, "showed me the wrong body down there in the morgue." "He did not," the Captain said firmly. "Why did you lie about it?" "I didn't, Captain. Honest!" "No? I suppose you have the nerve to stand there and tell me that you never heard of Wilbur Moore." "Come again, Captain," House replied, with what appeared to be honest perplexity written all over his face. "I mean Wilbur Moore-late of Folsom Prison," the Captain shouted. "Sure, I was there. When was this Moore there?" "In your time, House; and, further, we know for certain that you knew him in prison." "How news gets around," House commented airily. "Doesn't it?" the Captain agreed sarcastically. "But not through you!" "I always was the quiet one in the family, Captain." "You're going to be put in congenial surroundings then, House— 142

solitary confinement means quiet in my language. I've stood enough of your nonsense." "Eight years is a long time, Captain," the confidence man pleaded after a moment of silence. "You couldn't blame me for not recognizing him; he had changed a lot since I saw him." "So you did know him!" the Captain gloated. "Now, get along with the rest of the story-and get it right this time, if you know what's good for you." "That's all, Captain. I remember seeing him around the stir. I never really knew him." "If you had nothing to fear, House, what was your reason for not admitting you had known him in jail when you saw the body?" "Lots of detectives aren't as scrupulous as you are, Captain.

Some of them would've slapped a first degree murder charge on me just for knowing the deceased. I couldn't afford to have that happen." "There's still a good chance of that happening, if you don't set to and recall some useful details," the Captain threatened. "Hardly, Captain. You forget my alibi." "Completely, House. It doesn't even exist-if it ever did truthfully-so far as I'm concerned." "You'll never get away with that," House vowed. "And why not?" "My lawyer'll make you put your cards on the table, and you wouldn't want to show that mess of deuces and treys you hold right away." "They're as good as aces, if you have the ones that match, House.' 

"Sure, but you're color blind if you think yours hang together." "Oh, I guess we could get by the grand jury at least with our case right now, if we had to, House.' "Your case against whom, Captain?" "You." 143

"I said that I knew him; I didn't mention anything about killing him." "I didn't expect you would, House, but it hangs together nevertheless. One of your jail pals is murdered right across the hall from you, you conceal your knowledge of both the crime and the victim's identity, and then you kill off your alibi. That's about all we need for the present." "Go a little slower," House objected. "I didn't kill Helen Devere. Anyway, I still have my alibi. There are plenty of witnesses to that, if you can't believe me about Helen." "You saw to that," the Captain spat out. "Mind if I ask this heel a question or so, Captain?" the Sergeant broke in. 

Captain Wyckoff wheeled about in amazement. "Of course not, Sergeant. What's on your mind?" "Just a little surprise I told you this boid had comin' to him.” "Go ahead, then."

The Sergeant summoned his toughest manner and, waving a finger in front of House's nose, began: "Now, House, that alibi of yours ain't worth a plug nickel and you know it, ain't that so?" "What's eating you, Brass Toes?" House demanded indignantly.

The Sergeant's gruff voice continued: 

"I happen ta know that you and that Devere skoit went to the pitchers all right, and you made damned sure you was seen goin' into th' the-ater. But did anybody lamp you slidin' out that fire door about three-fifteen o'clock?" "Who saw that?" House asked innocently. "And then," the Sergeant continued, panting with excitement,- "and then, did anybody see you hot foot it over to your dump?" "I'll bite, Brass Toes; who did?" The last remark drew a faint smile from the Captain, but he wiped it away quickly and asked: "Is that true, Sergeant?" "As true as I'm a-standin' here." "Can't a fellow even go out for a glass of water without coming down here and getting permission first?" House asked carelessly. House spoke up confidently: "Captain, this is my third visit down here to Headquarters, so we may as well understand each other. I knew Moore in prison. I hadn't seen him since—that's eight years, you remember—until I saw the body in the morgue. I didn't kill him and I had no reason to want him put away for keeps. Further, you should have learned that I'm not the type that breaks down and tells all—just to suit a policeman. It won't do you any good to put me in your sweathouse." "No?" the Captain asked suggestively. "No," House echoed, "and besides you can't do it anyway." "You won't hear anything," the confidence man promised. "Perhaps not, but you'll remember for a long time that we asked -in a firm way-about it. Take him down, Sergeant. "No, not that way," he added hastily, as the Sergeant started toward the corridor door with House. "Take him out the side way. 

Ship him over to Station H and keep him out in the jungle. I don't want the reporters to know about this, or his lawyer will be in here 145

before the afternoon is over. Don't write up the arrest just yet either. 

Those reporters have a bad habit of looking at the blotter." "So, I'm being shanghaied," House shouted. "Just a little secret among the three of us," the Captain answered. "I hope that our confidence in you will be rewarded." "It won't," House snarled, as he was led out.

The Sergeant re-entered the office alone a few minutes later, scrubbing his huge hands together, as if to cleanse them of the job he had just done. I told th' boys not to waste no time with oratory, but to start right in shellacking." "Good. We can't count on keeping him under cover much longer than overnight. "Sure, all them mugs have their lawyers on full salary, Captain.' "Say, where did you pick up that tip, Mike? What's the idea of holding out important stuff like that?" "Didn't mean to, Captain. One of the stools picked it up and I didn't have no chance to give it to ya." "Okay, Mike. It's a help." "What about them Brisbanes down in the pressroom, Captain?" When they entered the Captain's office again, the police reporters found the detective captain a picture of despair and discouragement. 

Even the Mirror man, who had no great talents for reading the emotions of others, could see that the Captain's session with Jack House had been productive of little except hopelessness. 146

"Rotten luck, boys," the Captain moaned, with a plaintive sadness evident in his eyes. "We couldn't get a thing." "No oil, Captain," the Journal reporter admonished. "Did he admit knowing this Moore fellow?" "Yes, and that's all," the Captain replied. "Claims he knew

Moore only slightly in prison and swears that he hadn't seen him in eight years since he was released from Folsom. Of course, I can understand House's not wanting to take a trip back to the prison, but there was the time between Moore's release and his murder.

Ball. We'll turn you over to the advertising department this year." "Look at the blotter," the Captain advised patiently. "I can give you my word that House hasn't been booked." Never would get any police work done if we did." "You're too self-conscious," the Sergeant consoled his superior. "You ought to hear how them bum politicians lie to th' reporters.

"Precoisely. Goin' to give 'im a rub-down, too, like House?” "That depends."

George Curtis entered the Captain's office for the second time that day with fire in his eyes and his fists clenched in a nervous gesture. "Just what's the reason for this latest outrage of yours?" he blazed away at the Captain. "Meaning what, Mr. Curtis?" "What amounts to my second arrest today." "There must be some mistake," the Captain answered soothingly. "You aren't under arrest, Mr. Curtis. Why," he paused to laugh, "we hadn't even thought of that—as yet." "No? I might just as well be, for all the privacy I have left." "No, indeed," the Captain assured him. "However, since you are here, there are matters that we can discuss to our mutual benefit, I believe."

George clamped his thin lips firmly together and said defiantly: "I've told you all that I intend to tell." "Now, wait," the Captain responded casually. "Who is he?" "Wilbur Moore, as you could have told us this morning, had you been inclined to be cooperative." "I'm certain that we don't have to start with the ABC's in your case," the Captain resumed genially. "San Francisco is a big city, Captain. There were lots of people there I didn't know." "You knew Moore," the Captain said firmly. "I'll admit that I lived in San Francisco in 1930, Captain. So, of course, by police logic I knew Moore. Knowing Moore, naturally, I killed him. Having killed him, I dashed off to Chicago to cover up. I don't wonder there are so many bad jokes about the lack of intelligence in our police departments." "Sounds like House-vaguely, Sergeant," the Captain muttered. "At least credit me with enough sense not to run away, if I had killed a man," George continued. "We would be glad to, Mr. Curtis, if—"

The Captain stopped and stared coldly at George. "If," he resumed at last, "we weren't aware of your connection with Moore. There you have it! That's our only reason for invading your treasured privacy a second time today." "For my information," George inquired hastily, "just what is this supposed connection with Moore? I hadn't heard." "We'll get to that in due time," the Captain replied serenely. "Suppose you begin by explaining more precisely the nature of the business that took you to the Eagle Detective Agency in Chicago yesterday?" "I told you it was a private investigation in connection with one of the clients of our firm." "Yes, you did say that," the Captain conceded. Moore was in San Francisco and you were there as manager of the 

San Francisco branch for your firm. "We had to learn that for ourselves," the Captain continued reproachfully. "A mere detail," George answered sharply, with a dismissing gesture of his hands. I'm listening," George answered, beating out a rapid tattoo with his fingers on the edge of the desk. "You still aren't convinced that I know what I'm talking about, Mr. Curtis? San Francisco branch and borne-not by your firm-but by you personally."

George's fingers ceased their nervous drumming. He sighed deeply and stroked his chin reflectively. "How the devil did you know that, Captain?" he asked finally. "I didn't know for certain until this instant, but, now that I do, you may as well give me the details." 

Disgusted, George began to drum again. "As long as you've made a sap out of me, Captain, I'll provide the details. You could get them without great difficulty." "In no time," the Captain confirmed. "Here's the story then. I went out to San Francisco in 1920 and opened up the West Coast branch. It did well right from the start. 

By 1929 we were doing an important share of the firm's business. 

Moore was with us right from the start, first as a bookkeeper. He worked hard and long, and we knew nothing about his earlier prison record. We trusted him and advanced him gradually. In the boom days of '27 and '28 our organization was growing by leaps and bounds. Board boys were becoming paper millionaires 152

over night and leaving us to live in a style to which they'd never been accustomed. "Moore was the one stable employee; he stayed on, sober, industrious and apparently content to invest his savings in something besides the market. Just what, I never discovered nor cared. Finally, he became our cashier and handled large sums, both of the firm's money and our clients'." "Yes," the Captain supplied with an understanding nod. "He seemed to be completely trustworthy," George continued. "I was under tremendous pressure in those days and on into '29 and '30 when things went bad. I left more and more of the details in the hands of Moore. We reached the point where he really knew more about the actual handling of the firm's financial details than 

I did. . "Then," George resumed, after a pause for an audibly painful gulp, "he ran out on us, taking some $30,000 in readily-negotiable securities. The police caught up with Moore fast enough, but not before he had converted the securities into cash, and they never did recover the money. They sent him off to Folsom for a long stretch, but he never would tell what he had done with the money. You're right; I personally made good the loss and that was a hard blow.

I can sympathize with you; I had some of those Gromanians, too, and not just one thousand either." "Glad to know that the firm had that much confidence in them," the Captain remarked. "It makes me feel better about my loss." "Finally," George went on with his story, "the market got so bad in '31 and '32 that we practically closed up the Coast branch, although I continued on with a skeleton force, hoping for the eventual comeback until two years ago when we gave up the ghost and I came back East." "Just too early?" the Captain inquired pleasantly-"from what 

I hear about the market.” "Maybe we did guess wrong again." 153

"I wouldn't know," the Captain said. in "Anyway to get back to my story, Captain. Mr. Adams did save our necks when he came into the firm in 1932. He was a real lifesaver for all of us. There was considerable about it in the San Francisco papers for a few days at the time, but cashiers were absconding right and left in those days and $30,- 000 wasn't enough money to excite the New York papers. "That, then," George resumed, speaking more calmly, “brings us almost down to date. Naturally, I had been following Moore's career in prison with considerable interest. Moore's application for parole, and I knew the minute that he was released." "The Eagle Agency?" "Yes. You see, I figured that Moore still had that $30,000 somewhere. Knowing him, as I did, I couldn't convince myself that it had gone to pay market debts. He wasn't any other kind of a gambler either so far as I knew. He went right back to San Francisco, and I thought for awhile that my hunch had been a good one. your lost $30,000." "And you on the trail of "Right. Anyway, as soon as the agency reported that the trail had been lost, I rushed out to Chicago. business ethics. It isn't our conception-nor that of the nation, either—of a citizen's responsibility to his fellows-particularly when the reputations of his close friends are at stake, as they are in this case."

"I imagine," the Captain continued, "that you know liable to arrest for obstructing a police investigation." you are "Or for moider, foist degree," Sergeant Kearney shouted, speaking for the first time during the interview. "Why do they conceal facts?" George asked with interest. "Ask yourself, Mr. Curtis. Didn't you decline to identify Moore until you were compelled to admit knowing him?” "That was different, Captain. "And, to your mind, that's more important than bringing about the speedy arrest of a murderer. I might remind you that some people have lives at stake in this case. Two lives have been sacrificed already and others may be on the list of the man who is still at large, principally because you and your friends must have your little secrets."

Captain Wyckoff chose to ignore the question, and answered firmly: "I even doubt if your firm's drag in the better circles would be sufficient to gag us." "Meaning what?" "The story isn't finished,” the Captain said abruptly, “and it's bound to come out sooner or later." "I don't follow you, Captain." "I'm still waiting for an answer to my remarks about the motive and the opportunity." "Oh, that!" George answered carelessly. "Yes, that." "Let's have this straight, Captain."
