"Captain, I may have wished for Moore's death a thousand times in the last eight years, and I may have even expressed a desire to accomplish it," George replied jerkily, “but, irrespective of how much opportunity you may feel that I had, I didn't kill Moore, nor do I know who did. I wish that I did. I think that would provide the answer to what happened to my money. I'm convinced now that when you find the murderer, I'll learn where my money went." "Always, your money!" the Captain shouted. "Is there anything else that matters to you? Your friends? Your reputation?" "Money is important." “I can't put much faith in your disavowal under the circumstances, Mr. Curtis." "Doesn't my word mean anything to you?" George inquired in an alarmed tone. "Very little-after today." 

George's face turned pale, his hands trembled, and he almost shook with indignation. "Why-why-why," he stammered.

Abandoning words, he picked up his hat and stamped out of the office.

For the second time that day, Captain Wyckoff made no effort to 158

halt him. Instead he sat behind his desk and smiled serenely. 

Sergeant Kearney, however, looked like a seven-year-old boy who had just been told that he couldn't go to the circus. Crestfallen, he watched George Curtis rush from the office. "And now, why did yuh have to do that, Captain?" he moaned. "Yuh had him on th' ropes. He wouldn't have lasted five minutes longer. We could've picked th' bones and broke this case." "Mike, won't you ever learn that sometimes we have to use finesse in detective work? Besides, there are some people we can't run through the mill. This man represents interests too powerful to be rubber-hosed-unless you have a craving to go back to pounding a beat in Canarsie." "You don't have to do no thinkin' there," the Sergeant remembered. "All right, Mike, I'll do the thinking; you just be content with watching Curtis for a while longer."

The Sergeant's moods were typically Irish; they changed rapidly. 

In a moment he was smiling again. "Say, Captain," he declared admiringly, "you handled those boids great. The way you buffaloed them into spilling their guts. 

They didn't kid you none. I don't believe you had the drop on them a'tall." "Just suspicions," the Captain admitted modestly. 

"All that there inside dope about Curtis' firm was news to me,' the Sergeant complained. "Where'd you dig up all that doit?" "Remember that you put one over on me, too, Sergeant. I never heard that movie angle on House until you broke it." "Sure, but I was a'goin' to tell you, Captain." "Well, I had the business stuff looked up on the side. I didn't see any reason to clutter up your hard-working brain with it unless 

I found that it was going to be of some use to us.” "It sure was," the Sergeant marveled. "I'll bet that Curtis don't know yet what happened to him. And him, a tough business man!" 159

"I did enjoy that part of it," the Captain admitted with a broad grin. "Even if he isn't guilty of a thing, I certainly enjoyed taking him apart. I wish I knew if he told us the whole story." "Aw, you should'a done th' job on th' spot," the Sergeant lamented. "Why, you didn't even give him th' eye." "You have a lot of faith in 'the eye,' as you call it, Sergeant.' "And why not? We could'a dumped th' guy in no time." "Patience," the Captain counseled. "I'm a man of action," the Sergeant affirmed. "All right. Go into action then, and call those reporters back in here. This ought to redeem me some in their estimation for that lousy trick I played on them with House." "Don't trust no reporters, Captain," the Sergeant advised. "I won't, Sergeant, but I'll bet the story rocks all Wall Street." "Rocks! Hell, Captain. It'll panic 'em. The stocks'll go so sour them mugs'll wish Hoover was president again."

The Sergeant marched proudly toward the door, his Irish face beaming. 

The Captain stopped him: "Send that bootblack in, will you, Mike?" "What again?" "Yes, again." "Listen, Captain," the Sergeant remonstrated, "that's twice today already." 

The Captain laughed and answered: "Can't you understand, Mike? It makes me think better."

The Sergeant shook his head sadly. Obviously, he felt that his superior was beyond reformation. "Okay," he agreed. "Maybe Canarsie ain't so bad after all. At least you don't have to get yer kicks polished a dozen times a day, even if you don't do no thinkin' there." 160

CHAPTER 

ARTIN KENNEDY came home early that afternoon. 

Excitedly, he turned his key in the apartment door. "Lynn!" he shouted, flinging the door wide open. "Yes," a weary voice answered him. "Lynn! Have you seen the late editions of the afternoon papers?" 

Lynn raced out into the foyer. "What is it?" 

Martin handed over the Express with its screaming headlines:

SUTTON PLACE MURDER VICTIM IDENTIFIED AS EX-CONVICT; NEW DEATH CLUE, $30,000 THEFT, REVEALED BY N.Y. BROKER 

"George?" Lynn gasped with a swiftness that shocked Martin. 

She read rapidly down the column. Her face grew pale as she read.

Silently, she turned the pages and continued down to the last line of the story. Her eyes remained glued to the page long after she had finished reading. Gradually color returned to her cheeks, and a smile lighted up her large brown eyes and delicately-chiseled face. 

Lynn was beautiful when she smiled. 161

Then she spoke: "I wish you had a tenth of George's brains. He can handle a situation as a man should." 

Martin started to object: "Why 

But his wife stopped him: "I wish he had been here the night all this happened. That supercilious Captain would have been put in his place at once!" "But, Lynn • · 

"Never mind trying to excuse yourself, Martin. I'm all too accustomed to your deficiencies. I've come to expect them." 

Lynn sighed deeply, and continued heatedly: "What did you do to protect your wife when that prying detective came nosing into things? You were even afraid to speak to him. 

You hardly dared to call your soul your own as long as he was in the apartment.' "He's a policeman," Martin replied crisply. "He represents the law; he has a right . . ." "> "Yes, and speaking of rights," his wife interrupted, "what . .

She stopped suddenly, as the telephone rang sharply, only to resume as Martin walked to answer it: "... of me? Where do my rights come in?"

Only half hearing, Martin picked up the receiver. He listened for a moment. "Please," he said, "speak more distinctly. I can't understand you." 

Patiently, he listened again. 

Once more he asked, "Please speak more clearly. I can't hear you." 

He listened intently. "Oh, it's Mrs. Kennedy, you wish. Just a moment, please. It's for you, dear." 

Lynn hissed, "Who is it?" 162

"I don't know," Martin whispered. "It's a most peculiar-sounding voice. It sounds far away. I could barely make it out.” "Oh, well, give me the phone!"

Martin handed over the receiver and returned to the livingroom divan. "Hello," Lynn called, and a minute later, "Hello." 

Turning her head, "There isn't a soul on here, Martin." "Listen closely," her husband advised. 

Lynn called again, "Hello" and waited. Finally Martin heard "Yes, this is Mrs. Kennedy." her say, And, after a pause: "What? What did you say?"

There was silence again, punctured by a chilling shriek from Lynn. She dropped the receiver on the floor and stood as if dazed by an unexpected blow. 

Martin rushed to the telephone, and clapped the receiver to his ear. He heard only the steady buzz which indicated that the connection had been broken. 

He turned to Lynn: "What was it? What frightened you?" 

Lynn did not answer at once. She stood silently, obviously steeling herself. Gradually, her breathing became more normal and it was apparent that she had adjusted herself to whatever shock she had received over the telephone. "What was it?" Martin pleaded. "Nothing," his wife said off-handedly. "Nothing you need worry about." "But.. >> 

Lynn acted as if she had not heard. They sat in the livingroom for several minutes without saying a word.

There was a wide stare in Lynn's eyes that indicated that her thoughts were far away from the room in which she sat. 

Suddenly, her head slumped forward and she began to cry convul- 163

sively. Her entire body shook and tears flowed down her cheeks in steady streams. "I'm frightened," she whispered. "About what?" Martin asked. 

Again Lynn said nothing for some time. Finally, she answered: "Nothing that you would understand. Your dinner is ready; I don't want any."

Martin ate his dinner alone and silently while Lynn sat in a corner of the livingroom. When Martin had finished, she said: "I want to go out-anywhere. Just to be out of this apartment for a few hours." "We could, of course," Martin agreed. “Where do you want to go?" "I don't know. Don't you ever have an original idea—a suggestion to offer of any kind? Must I do all the planning?" "We could go see the Curtises," Martin suggested tentatively. 

The suggestion seemed to delight Lynn.

Lynn and Martin took the elevator a few minutes later up to apartment 17D. 

Marian appeared curiously light-hearted and gay, in view of all the perplexing happenings of the day. "Greetings," she called. "Glad you dropped in. George is consoling himself with whisky and soda. He's been consoling himself ever since six o'clock and he's still just about as mad as when he first came home from police headquarters." "Those detectives!" he exclaimed. "I know," Martin sympathized. "Was it bad?" "Awful," George answered, with a shudder. "They get you so completely confused that you don't know right from left. It's over, let's forget it. How about some of this?" he suggested, tapping his glass. 

The Kennedys agreed readily. "Well, you two certainly thrive on adversity," Lynn remarked. 164

"Why?" George asked. "I thought that we'd find you both down in the dumps, but I don't believe I ever saw either of you looking so well before, or so happy." "A clear conscience," George explained. "I'm glad that Captain 

Wyckoff knows the whole story now." "But . . ." Lynn objected, only to stop the second the word was out of her mouth. "Why in the world didn't you tell him right at the start?" Martin inquired earnestly. "Remember that I didn't arrive home until this morning, Martin." "That's so," Martin assented quietly. "It seems as if the murder occurred years ago instead of the day before yesterday." "But, Marian. to stop abruptly. ." again Lynn started to say something, only 

George stepped into the kitchen. "Hurry those drinks along," Marian called quickly. Martin noticed that she shifted her eyes away from Lynn, and that was another thing he couldn't understand. 

George returned shortly with a tray of glasses. "Do you think your story will help the police in arriving at a solution of the case?" Martin asked his host. "Hard to tell. I suppose that it may. Blast that Eagle Agency; they let Moore slip right through their fingers. They'll never get any more business whatsoever from me, regardless of the circumstances." >> "I hope you never have another occasion to hire a detective agency, ” Marian said in a quietly determined tone. "Cheer up, George," Lynn suggested, "maybe you'll collect now." 

George smiled and answered hopefully, "I might. I have a hunch that I may." 165

"I ought to rate a fur cape for having the creature killed in our apartment," Lynn said brightly. "You do," George promised gaily. "Silver fox, too." "Why can't we talk of something besides this ghastly murder?" 

Marian inquired with a pout. "Now, don't try to change the subject on me before I get this silver fox down in black and white," Lynn persisted. "Oh, George never forgets little things like that," Marian answered carelessly. "You have my word," George affirmed, "and before witnesses, too. I'm tired of the subject of murder also. How about playing some bridge?" 

Lynn agreed happily: "It's a crazy thing to do and I suppose that the tabloids, if they knew about it, would cite it as an illustration of the 'vapid intellectual life of the idle rich.' Incidentally, Martin," she added, "if this were anything but a murder case, I'd get a lot of enjoyment out of being called one of the ‘idle rich.' But, then, we never did play that rubber with Gerry and Blair." 

The bridge game went forward at a desultory pace. None of the four players was greatly interested. Still, it was probably better than just talking.

Marian had difficulty in remembering trumps and several times found herself slipping into what seemed to be a sort of coma in the middle of a hand and was totally unable to remember which cards had been played. 

George reneged twice, and didn't apologize. 

Lynn mis-spoke and called spades when she meant to bid hearts. 

Martin played more falteringly and with less decision than Lynn had ever seen him. He even forgot to make bold bids whenever the opportunity offered itself.

By common consent the bridge game broke up about ten o'clock and the Kennedys prepared to leave. 166

"I'm off to bed," George announced, as they said good night at the door, "as soon as I take Susie for a short walk down to the river." "I'm glad I don't own any dogs," Lynn said firmly. "I think it's ridiculous having to take them out every morning and night.” "It would be worse if you didn't," George said. "I'd stick to a cat, if I had to have a pet in a New York apartment," Lynn answered. "Good night!"

The Kennedys entered the elevator, and a few minutes later George struggled into his topcoat and picked up his hat. "Susie, get your lead," he instructed the dog. Quivering with delight, Susie raced into the kitchen, and galloped back into the livingroom dragging the lead in her mouth.

George leaned down to adjust the lead and pounded the dog playfully in the ribs. Susie yelped with pleasure and pawed at 

George's chin. "Coming along?" George asked Marian. "I don't believe so. I don't feel like it-unless you want me to, particularly." "No, dear. We'll be back in a few minutes. Come along, Susie." 

Together they went down Fifty-fifth Street to Sutton Place

South and out on the East River pier at Fifty-fourth Street. 

The dock there is unlighted and usually deserted after dark except for an occasional dog walker from one of the large apartment houses in the vicinity. The entire block, in fact, is dark, foreboding, and unfrequented. The west side of the block is occupied by a longdeserted brewery, the river side by walled-in tennis courts, and to the south there is nothing except a large vacant area used for the storage of sand and gravel. Beyond are abandoned tenements and steam laundries and garages. By day, it was a scene of quiet city activity; by night, a lonely river front.

George stood admiring the East River from the darkened pier while Susie rustled about in the sand piles. He had a sincere affec- 167

tion for the East River; its brooding blackness reminded him of its powerful and varying currents, of its depth and treachery, and, most of all, of its compelling majesty. The Hudson was probably far more beautiful in the eyes of most people, George mused. Its sunrises and sunsets were famous wherever people discussed those great natural spectacles, but George liked the East River. Its beauty was a black beauty-the poetry of mud, struggling tugs, and rushing waters.

So absorbed was George in his reverie that he didn't heed the faint creaking of the heavy pier flooring as a crouching figure crept up behind him. Susie barked suddenly and sprang toward her master. George tried to turn his head-too late. A heavily-dealt blow caught him in the back of the head and he slumped slowly to the pier, enveloped in a blackness as dark as that of the river. 

Just then the lights of an automobile flashed into Fifty-fourth Street as a car turned in from First Avenue, a block away. A heavy object splashed into the river and George's assailant slipped away into the shadows of the sand and gravel piles. 

A little black dog, barking crazily, halted the motorist as he reached the waterfront. Susie stood in the middle of the street, yelping her loudest. As the car stopped and the driver stepped out, the dog dashed toward the pier, stopped, turned about, barked impatiently and insistently, demanding to be followed. The motorist hesitated. Then, grabbing a flashlight from a side pocket in his car, he followed the dog out to the pier. Near the water's edge, his light picked up the inert form of George Curtis, who only a minute earlier had stood there meditating on rivers and beauty. 

The motorist retreated to his car and drove madly back to First Avenue, where, with rare luck, he found a policeman on the corner. 

He sped back to the pier with the patrolman. Together they bent over George Curtis, with Susie hovering about, yelping plaintively. 

Expertly, the policeman reached for George's wrist. He felt a faint and irregular pulse. He nodded. 168

"Drive back to that corner drugstore and telephone for an ambulance!" he ordered.

As the motorist ran toward his car, Patrolman Stevens, for he was Maurice Stevens, shield No. A47-632, continued his hasty examination. He noted a rapidly-rising bump at the base of George's skull. His superficial examination showed no other serious injury, however. 

Then, George's eyelids began to flutter and he moved his head slowly from side to side, trying to shake off the black mist that still froze his brain. "What happened?" he asked, regaining consciousness with a final vigorous shake of his head. "Someone bumped you over the head, I guess."

George attempted to rise and the policeman assisted him over to the bulkhead where he sat down. "How do you feel? Okay?" "I guess so," George answered undecidedly, holding his head with both hands. "Have you any idea who or what hit you?" "I thought I heard someone creeping up behind me and then 

Susie-that's my dog," he explained, "barked to warn me. Too late," he recalled hazily. "How long have I been out?" "I don't know. Your dog stopped a car. Very bright dog," the patrolman commented, "and the driver raced up to the corner to pick me up. I don't think it could have been very long." 

He pulled out his watch. "It's ten-twenty-two now, if that means anything to you.' "It was only a short time, then. It was less than ten minutes ago that I left my house," George remembered. "It seems hours," he groaned. "What's your name?" Patrolman Stevens went on matter-offactly. "George V. Curtis." 169

"Where do you live?" "706 East Fifty-fifth." "That sounds familiar," Patrolman Stevens remarked, pushing his cap back from his forehead in order to scratch his temple more effectively. "You're the broker that bird stole the thirty grand from!" the policeman exclaimed. "Well, it's a sure thing he didn't try to nick you again tonight."

He laughed loudly, but stopped almost in the middle of a guffaw: "Jees! I had better get word to Captain Wyckoff right away." "Again?" George groaned. "What's that?" the patrolman demanded sharply. Stevens-S-t-e-v-e-n-s-now don't forget it; that part is important, too." "Yes, for you," George mumbled. 170

"Did you meet anyone you knew when you went out tonight," the Captain asked. "Only the night elevator man." "Did anyone know just when you were going out?" "Why, only the Kennedys." "The Kennedys?" the Captain exclaimed. “Yes,” Marian explained. "They were here earlier. They left about ten, and, as I remember it, George remarked that he was going to take Susie out for a walk before going to bed." "That's right," George affirmed. "I remember that we were talking about dog-walking and Lynn said that she would never own anything except a cat. She was quite vindictive about it, too." 

The Captain jumped to his feet. "Mr. Curtis, if you had an ounce of brains, you would have told me that down on the pier. Come on, Sergeant."

They raced from the apartment before George or Marian could say another word. 183

CHAPTER 

9 C APTAIN WYCKOFF rang the down-button persistently. 

He jabbed at it four times before he heard the mollifying closing of an elevator door floors below, and the clank of safety chains as the car began its ascent. Still, he didn't relax. He limped nervously back and forth in front of the elevator door on the seventeenth floor-a victim of the modern skyscraper where nine floors are too many to walk down, even for a man in a hurry. 

He stopped only once to mutter to the Sergeant, "Maybe this is what we've been waiting for, Mike." 

The Sergeant nodded grimly.

They jumped into the elevator when it reached their floor, and the Captain directed: "Eight, quick!"

The night elevator man yawned and started the car downward. "Have Mr. and Mrs. Kennedy returned?" the Captain asked.

The operator stifled another yawn with great difficulty, and answered, "Huh?" "When did Mr. and Mrs. Kennedy come in?" the Captain repeated. 

The elevator operator shrugged his shoulders. "Won't this car travel no faster?" the Sergeant demanded, 184

although the elevator was a modern one and descending at a good speed. "No," was the operator's flat answer. "Then, hurry it, buddy," the Sergeant advised sternly.

At the eighth floor the two detectives sprang from the elevator and raced across the corridor to the Kennedy apartment. Despite his years and bad leg, the Captain led by half a step and pressed the apartment bell determinedly. 

There was no answer. Again and again, the Captain rang, but got no response. He turned the doorknob, and the door opened as he touched it. The Kennedys' apartment was ablaze with lights. 

Cautiously, the Captain and the Sergeant entered, the Sergeant first stopping to slip his service revolver from its holster and to release the safety catch.

The livingroom was empty... the bedroom also. They searched the rest of the apartment, but found no one. A hat, evidently Martin's, rested on the arm of a chair in the foyer. Finally, they came back to the foyer closet where Moore's body had been found.

The Sergeant examined his revolver carefully, and slipped his middle finger over the trigger. Slowly, the Captain opened the door. His flashlight revealed . . . a vacuum cleaner, a bridge table, a mop and a pail. 

Disgustedly, the Captain slammed the door shut. "We can't expect to find them in the same place twice," he muttered. 

The telephone rang urgently.

At a nod from the Captain, the Sergeant picked up the receiver, and, after neatly folding a not-too-clean handkerchief over the mouthpiece, whispered: "Hello." 

He listened intently. Again, he called softly: "Hello." 185

After listening for a few minutes more, he banged the receiver hook up and down furiously. "Operator! Operator!" he demanded. "Trace that call at once!" "One mom-ent, ple-ase, I will give you the Chief Operator." "Aw, hell," the Sergeant shouted, and slammed the receiver back in place. "Them dames give me a pain. No co-operashun." "Well?" the Captain inquired patiently.

The Sergeant sighed deeply and stopped to mop the perspiration from his forehead before replying: "The lug was covering the phone. I couldn't get a word, Captain." 

Captain Wyckoff laughed. "Well, Mike, our little friend may have a dose of telephonitis, but at least he isn't stupid about it."

The Captain limped away, and settled himself carefully in an easy chair in the livingroom. Without seeming to be making the slightest choice, he picked one that faced the foyer and commanded a good view of the door into the public corridor. He relaxed and stretched out comfortably. "What's up?" the Sergeant asked. "You don't figger to take no nap now, do you, chief?" "Far from it, Mike. Now, if you'll just snap off all those lights before you go. "Before I go ?" "Right, Mike. Before you go." "But, Captain · • "I know, Mike. This has become a two-man job, and I'm picking this nice soft chair for my part of the job." "Okay, Captain, but . . " "No 'buts,' Mike. See you later."

The Sergeant extinguished all the lights. As he stood at the corridor door, the Captain directed: 186

"Leave it unlocked the way we found it." "Okay," the Sergeant replied, and softly closed the door, leaving the Captain sitting alone in the dark apartment. 

The Sergeant stopped in the public corridor indecisively. "What th' hell," he muttered finally. "It can't do no harm.”

His mind made up, he stepped briskly to the apartment of Jackson Hatteras. He rang the bell, but there was no answer. 

Undismayed, he turned across the hall and tried the Eubanks'. 

Again there was no reply. "I don't suppose the old dame is home, either," he murmured, walking to Dr. Ross' door. She was not.

Methodically, the Sergeant made a series of notes in his little memorandum book, snapped it back into his hip pocket with the verdict: "Jeez! What home bodies."

He glanced at the floor indicator over the elevator door and noticed that the car was coming up. He waited without ringing, and the car stopped at the eighth floor. 

It was the Taylor Eubanks.

The Sergeant swept off his derby with a flourish.

Martin looked down, surprised. He tossed away the cigaret and started downtown again.

A Staten Island ferryboat plowed through a rolling sea past the Statue of Liberty on her New York run. A chilling wind swept across her bow, and her few passengers huddled in the cabins, reading tabloids and yawning.

Dr. Hildegarde Ross stood alone on the deserted open deck close to the boat's prow. The salty spray whipped across her face. "It's marvelous," she whispered to herself. "So bracing. There's nothing like it after a hard day's work. It's just a pity that more 

New Yorkers don't realize what it does for one."

The ferryboat bumped into her berth, and Dr. Ross walked ashore with a spirited step. 

Aloft in the pilothouse, the Captain remarked to his visitor: "There's one of our regulars. She takes the trip, just for the ride, three or four times a week-winter or summer. Don't tell me this isn't a pleasure boat I run." 

Up in Apartment 8B, Captain Leander Wyckoff had dozed off.

Snorts and resounding snores interrupted his regular, slow breathing. 

The telephone rang, shattering the quiet. Instantaneously, the

Captain was awake and moving toward the receiver. 

As cautiously as the Sergeant had earlier, he draped his handkerchief over the mouthpiece and said quietly, "Hello."

He heard nothing except a metallic click as a receiver was replaced on its hook on the other end of the wire.

The Captain called "Hello" again, and knew that the wire had gone dead. 192

Returning to the livingroom, he sank back into his chair and took up his vigil.

He glanced at his watch. The hands stood at twelve-twenty. Then the Captain fell asleep again.

Sometime later he awakened as a key was turned in the alreadyunlocked door of the Kennedy apartment. The Captain sat waiting quietly as the lights were snapped on in the foyer. 

Lynn Kennedy sat uncomfortably against a stone wall which projected from the bank of the East River along one side of the pier at Fifty-fifth Street. Idly, she watched the flashlights of the

Sergeant's men a block south as they continued to pick their way silently around the sand yard and here and there through the deserted brewery.

She gazed out at the black, oily water before her. She discovered a certain friendly quality in its lonesomeness. As George Curtis had a few hours earlier, she began to sense its grandeur. A tug puffed cheerily up the river, dragging four large railroad barges by invisible cables. Infrequently an automobile or an elevated train rattled across the Fifty-ninth Street Bridge to the north. Apartment house lights were snapped out, one by one. Sutton Place was going to bed.

Lynn lost herself in contemplation of the soothing swish of the river. Time passed. How much, she had no way of knowing. Unexpectedly, she heard an unconcealed creaking of the board flooring of the pier near her. There were footsteps, and a single ray of light crept slowly around the pier, as if seeking her out. Too quickly, it slid along the stone wall, trapped and blinded her. 

She heard a man's voice say: “Well, I'll be—” 

The voice sounded familiar. 193

It went on: "And now what might you be a'doin' here, Mrs. Kennedy? 

Watchin' an oily sunrise?"

Lynn's eyes failed her, and she could see nothing against the sudden light, but she knew it was Sergeant Kearney speaking. "Hardly, Sergeant," she replied coolly. "I'm just getting a little air. Please turn that light out of my face." "A little light on things won't do no harm," the Sergeant argued.

Nevertheless, he lowered the light. The Sergeant probably had absorbed, by close association alone, some of the gentlemanly instincts of his superior officer, although it's doubtful if he ever would have admitted that. As the flash was lowered, its rays gleamed for a brief moment on Lynn's tan pumps. Except for the straps over the arches and the leather above the high heels, the tan was veiled by a dirty gray coating. 

The Sergeant found his voice with a mighty roar: "So! That's the yarn! Just gittin' a little air! "Would yuh mind telling me, Mrs. Kennedy, if it ain't too much trouble," he added extravagantly, "whether yuh bounce down here every night at one a.m. all alone jest to soak up a little air?" "That's no concern of yours," Lynn answered firmly. "And, Mr. Kennedy," the Sergeant went on, with hardly a pause for breath, "was he detained elsewhere on other business, or is he about the premises somewheres too?" "Why don't you look around for him?" Lynn suggested. 

The Sergeant ignored Lynn's careless answer and shouted: "Now, yuh wouldn't be down here a'lookin' for somethin' that's heavier than air, would yuh?" "Why, no. What would I be looking for?" CHAPTER 

10 "HERE was a decided nip in the autumn air the next morn- ing, and steam hissed cheerily as it escaped from a defective valve in the Curtis apartment. 

There was no need for a fire in the open fireplace as well, yet Marian Curtis sat on the hearth feeding kindling into the tiny blaze before her. As the kindling caught, she put on split logs until a roaring fire blazed.

Marian watched for some time, her face a study in contrasting frowns as if she were turning over a weighty problem in her mind. 

Finally, she arose, walked to a desk, took a batch of letters from a drawer, and returned to her place on the hearth. Slowly and carefully, she dropped the letters into the flames, letting each one burn completely and breaking up the tiny charred remains with an iron poker before dropping in the next. When the last letter was gone, she stood up, brushed the ash from her fingers, and patted Susie on the head. "That's that. Now, don't you tell, Susie," she said softly. "Give this th' eye, Captain," Sergeant Kearney shouted as he burst into Captain Wyckoff's office about the same time, holding a cutlass-like weapon in his hand. 204

The Captain took the odd-shaped sword and examined it carefully. "We scrubbed off th' mud," the Sergeant explained. 

Apparently oblivious to what his subordinate was saying, the detective Captain continued to scrutinize the curved sword. "A Filipino bolo," he muttered. "It's curious, Mike." "We pulled it outta th' East River a good broad jump out from th' dock at Fifty-fourth Street." "It couldn't be," the Captain mumbled. “Still—” "It's gotta be," the Sergeant insisted. "It'd be all rusty if it'd been in the drink a long time." "But, why a bolo, when a length of iron pipe would have been just as effective and less expensive?” the Captain inquired. Lynn looked worn and nervous. It was apparent that she had not slept much and the pallor that she'd carried for three nights seemed to have become a permanent part of her makeup. York's best legal talent was routed out of bed last night. Your husband-and another whose name I need not mention, are doing very best they possibly can, I assure you." the "But, I'm still here." Detective Sergeant Michael Kearney then noticed that Detective

Captain Leander Wyckoff was paying no attention to what he was saying. The Captain was leaning forward on his elbows, his hands planted behind his ears and a vacant stare in his eyes. The cast seemed to move in his left eye, but otherwise his eyes were motionless.

Sergeant Kearney knew the symptoms. The Captain was engaged in mulling over the case, or "crystal-gazing," as he called it in his less professional moments. The Sergeant sat quietly by to await the inevitable questions, to which the Captain would expect no answers, just reassurance.

In the meantime, he knew that the happenings of the last three days were parading through the Captain's orderly mind in a varied procession—a murder, a suicide, a disappearance, an attack on one of the principals, an arrest, a mystifying disregard for the complete truth on the part of several persons. Slowly and carefully, he would array the known facts in his mind. Idly, as if trying to piece together a jig-saw puzzle, he would group them, first one way, then another. He would try a third, a fourth, and as many other combinations as he could imagine. Then, at last, he would tell the Sergeant what it was all about. At least, that was the way that it had always happened in the other murder cases on which they'd worked together in the past. 

The Sergeant waited patiently. It always took a long time, but never before had it required so much time. Finally, the Captain lifted his head, sighed and reached for his briar. "It's no use, Mike. It won't think out. The key piece is still missing. We don't know enough yet." 209

"Stuck, Captain?" "Completely, Mike. What we know so far won't stick together any way, and I've tried everything that I can think up." "I guess we gotta do some more woik, Captain." "Sure, Mike, that would be it nine times out of ten, and it probably is here too, but try to make it fit in. Who got the money?" "I dunno, Captain." "Whose money was it?" "Prob❜bly Curtis'." CHAPTER 

S 

ERGEANT KEARNEY bustled into Captain Wyckoff's of fice. "We've got the Kennedys' dinge, Captain," he reported. "I'm shootin' right up to Harlem now." "Wait a minute, Mike, and I'll go along with you."

Together they drove quickly to a shabby old brownstone house in West One Hundred Thirty-eighth Street. A uniformed policeman stood in the first floor hallway of the house. "Third floor, Captain," he directed, saluting. The hall steps were bare and worn and the walls stripped of whatever covering they had once had. The stairs creaked pitifully as if the thousands of footsteps they had endured had been too much for them to bear, and evidences of minor sabotage of the kind inflicted by children with paint brushes, knives, matches, and crayons were everywhere.

The musty odor of meals long since consumed hung in the atmosphere, and a dozen varieties of insects dashed crazily about the stairs. "Phew!" the Captain exclaimed. "These places spoil my appetite for days. Glad I don't live here."

Another patrolman admitted them to the third floor flat. They found themselves in a livingroom, which was cheaply and garishly furnished but scrupulously neat and clean in startling contrast with 224

the dingy public hall outside. Beyond lay a gloomy hall running back through the flat with tiny cell-like rooms, each large enough for a cot and dresser, leading off it. Like most Harlem flats, it evidently accommodated a number of persons, each with his little cell for a bedroom and sharing the livingroom as a common room. "A swell joint to hide out in," the Sergeant observed as a detective shouted, "Come out here, Gloria."

Gloria Brown appeared in the doorway of the livingroom almost at once, from the direction of the row of little black cells. "This is Captain Wyckoff, Gloria," the detective said in a kindly voice. "He wants to hear your story, just as you told it to me. 

Don't be frightened; he won't hurt you." "Cap'n, please, you won't put me in the jailhouse?" Gloria pleaded. "Of course not, Gloria. Why did you run away?" "Thanks, Cap'n," the maid answered gratefully. "Ah was scared. 

But, ah didn't run away. No, suh! Ah didn't know you was lookin' fur me." "Now, Gloria, you know that's not the truth," the Captain admonished. "Swear it tis, Cap'n," Gloria insisted. "Minute ah heared you was huntin' fur me, ah woulda come right to th' jailhouse. No, suh, you didn't have to come and fetch me." "It doesn't matter, Gloria," the Captain replied patiently. "We didn't want to arrest you; we just wanted to know what frightened you so badly. What did?” "Ah seen that man in th' closet. Ah was scared he was dead, so ah run." "What man, Gloria?" "Why, the dead man, Cap'n. How do you suppose ah got scared?" "We don't know, Gloria." "Why, sure, Cap'n. Ah seen that man and ah lit out. 'Course, 225

ah had to meet up with that no-account Magnolia trash what works fur the white folks next door. Ah tells her not to tell on me, so right off that riff-raff blabs it out to you all." "She was frightened, Gloria, or I don't believe she would have told us," the Captain said in the interests of future peace in Harlem. "None th' less, ah takes care of her in ma own way in ma own time," Gloria vowed. "Why did you go to Mrs. Kennedy's apartment, Gloria? You had no business there, did you?" "Yas, ah did, Cap'n." 

"What?" the Captain asked expectantly. "Ah had to git that iron, Cap'n." 

Captain Wyckoff's eyes widened. "What iron do you mean, Gloria?" "Why, that ironin' iron, 'course, Cap'n. What iron you all think ah means? There ain't but that one." 

The Captain roared. "I thought for a minute you were talking about the bolo with which Moore was killed," he explained when he had regained control of his face. "Ah don't know nuthin' about that lo-bo, Cap'n," Gloria protested earnestly. "What's that?" "You remember the curved sword that hung on the wall in the foyer?" the Captain inquired. "Oh, yas, Cap'n, ah rec-lects that sword." "Well, that is called a bolo, Gloria." "Sakes 'live," Gloria muttered, showing inches of white eyeball. "Ah never 'magined that." "Could you recall, Gloria, if that bolo was in its usual place the wall when you entered the apartment?" was the Captain's next question. "Ah don't know, Cap'n. Ah didn't wait to find out. Ah seen 226

that man and ah ran, just like ah told you. Ah wasn't stoppin' to look at no swords or oboes." "Too bad," the Captain murmured. "Now, Gloria, I want you to start at the beginning and tell us everything that happened. Do you understand?" "Yas, suh, ah does," Gloria responded brightly. "Well, it was lik' this. That afternoon ah was working for Mis' Nicholson over in the other side of th' house, like I always does." "She is also a tenant in the apartment house where Mrs. Kennedy lives?" the Captain interrupted. "That's ke-rect, Cap'n. Now, as ah was sayin', ah was workin' for Mis' Nicholson that afternoon. Ah was ironin' that lovely red satin kimona of hers when the iron went 'pouff' and blew out.

Well, Mis' Nicholson wanted that kimona ironed bad, so ah says, 'Don' you weep none, Mis' Nicholson, ah'll fetch an iron from

Mis' Kennedy's and fix it.' 'Course, ah knows all th' time that Mis' 

Kennedy won't 'ject none, so ah goes fur that iron. Ah opens the door with my pass key and goes to that closet in th' hall. 'Course, I don't suspicion nuthin'. Believe me, ah takes one look and slams shut th' door. Then, ah flies right out of that place quick. Sufferin' angels, was ah skeert!” "I know," Captain Wyckoff said sympathetically. "But why in the world did you run away and hide here?” "Cap'n, this here is th' truth as sure as ah'm standin' here, ah was 'fraid you all would accuse me ob killin' that man and ah didn't do that.” "We know you didn't kill anyone, Gloria." "Praise the Lor'," Gloria replied fervently, "becuz, ah didn't do that." "Now, Gloria," the Captain continued, "you're certain that you're telling us the exact, unvarnished truth?" "Yas, suh, Cap'n. What for should ah make up stories 'bout that?" 227

"You weren't in the apartment when the man was killed there?" "Deed, ah wasn't, Cap'n. If ah'd been there then, ah would have left quicker than scat." "I'm certain you would have," the Captain responded with a grin, "but I wanted to be positive. Do you know Mr. and Mrs. Curtis?" "Yas, suh, ah does." "You didn't see them in the apartment?" "Cap'n, ah didn't see nobody in th' 'partment—except that dead man, an' ah didn't stop to hold no con-ver-sah-sion with him a-tall." "Outside the apartment?” “No, suh, ah didn't see nobody outside either, except that trashy colored person from next door." "Where have you been these three days, Gloria?" "Right here, mindin' mah own business." "No one told you to move and go in hiding?" "No, suh, that was mah own idea," Gloria answered proudly. "I don't understand why you ran away then." "Ah was 'fraid, Cap'n." "Of what?" "That man-he was dead."

The mention of Moore seemed to indicate the futility of the approach the Captain was attempting and to suggest another. "Had you ever seen that man around the Kennedy apartment previously?" he asked. "No, Cap'n, ah never seen him nowheres." "You could swear to that?" "Cross my heart and spit in yuh eye, Cap'n." "That will do," the Captain concluded, a definite gleam of satisfaction in his eye. "Now, Gloria, the trouble is almost over. You can move back to your old quarters, if you want to. No one is going to arrest you, but next time, remember, don't try to hide from the 228

police because they'll always find you in the end and it looks suspicious to hide." "Yas, suh, Cap'n. Ah won't. Praise the Lor', ah's freed agin," she replied devoutly. 

Captain Wyckoff smiled indulgently and turned to the Sergeant: "Let's get back downtown, Mike. I've an idea that things are going to begin happening before long." "Begin to happen!" the Sergeant exclaimed. "Whatta yuh think's been goin' on for the last three days-a sewing coicle?" "Don't be so literal, Mike. I mean that the end of this mess is just about in sight now." "A pinch?” the Sergeant asked joyfully. "The pinch!" the Captain answered confidently. "When?" "Shortly," the Captain announced enigmatically. "I don't git it a-tall," the Sergeant complained. "You will soon," his superior promised. "I'm not yet positive myself, but I think we're on the right track at last. It's really so simple." 

The Sergeant brightened under the spell of the Captain's confidence: "Sure, Captain, these cases is always a pipe for us. It's only a question of time before we put th' bee on the right guy. We never failed yet, did we, Captain?" "Never," the Captain agreed. 

They motored swiftly back downtown to Headquarters, and the

Captain took up a vigil at his desk. The Sergeant looked over the reports from his men. There was nothing of unusual interest. The 

Captain appeared completely imperturbed and singularly uninterested in what the reports contained. He yawned luxuriantly, slowly patted his open mouth with his hand, and drawled: "Well, Mike, maybe tonight we can go back to sleeping again.

These murder cases always set me back about two weeks." 229

Before Sergeant Kearney could think of a suitable reply, the interoffice telephone rang. "Send him in," the Captain directed over the wire. "A caller," he informed the Sergeant vaguely. "Is this it?" the Sergeant asked anxiously. "No, not yet. Keep your shirt on," his superior advised.

Gerry West sauntered into the office. Nonchalant in manner, the reporter appeared to be attempting to make himself seem more carefree than he probably felt. "Hello, Captain. Hi, Sergeant. I see where somebody biffed good old George last night. A fine police department you fellows run!

It's getting so no decent citizen can walk a dog without landing in a mess of trouble. I never thought that I'd live to see the day when dog walking had to be listed as a hazardous occupation.”

The Sergeant sniffed his displeasure, but the Captain was cordial. "Hello, West. Where have you been lately?" "The Sergeant's minions ought to be able to give you a fairly complete report, Captain. I stumble over two or three of them every morning when I open my apartment door to pick up the Times.

The elevator started up. With his consummate flair for the dra- 241

matic, the Captain declined to name a floor. Charlie, with his complete desire to have as little as possible to do with policemen, declined to ask. Slowly, the elevator rose toward an unnamed destination. 

Two-three-four-five-six-seven. . 

Then, the Captain broke the silence: "The next one will do, Charlie." The Captain turned left toward the Kennedys' apartment, halted dramatically before the door and turned to grin at the Sergeant, who was busily engaged in unlimbering his service revolver. As a broad beam of satisfaction covered the Irish face of his Sergeant, Captain Wyckoff wheeled and marched stiffly to the door of apartment 8A, the home of Dr. Hildegarde Ross. 

Sergeant Kearney's usually inscrutable face drooped. "Jeez, Captain. Not her!" 

The Captain smiled lightly and pressed the bell. 

Dr. Ross answered the ring at once. She greeted the Captain graciously: "Calling so soon again, Captain? Really, not a thing has come up." "May we come in anyway?" the Captain asked. 

"Why. of course certainly," she answered slowly, her eyes racing over the faces of the Captain and the Sergeant, "but .. The two detectives followed Dr. Ross into the livingroom. 

Then, in a modulated voice, as if making a most casual remark, the Captain announced: "Dr. Ross, you are under arrest for the murder of Wilbur Moore."

The psychiatrist dropped into a chair and fainted. The Captain motioned the Sergeant to replace his revolver. "Water," he ordered.

He held the glass to her lips, and after a few minutes the doctor regained her composure. 242

"I guess this is the end," she murmured slowly. "I've dreaded this moment for three days. Yet, I knew that it had to come sometime." 

She aroused herself further and continued in that slow, sure voice of hers, "Still, it isn't as bad as I had pictured it in my mind, and 

I am not sorry," she concluded defiantly. "That will come later," the Captain said solemnly. "However, we know more about the circumstances than you think we do." 

Sergeant Kearney stood mopping perspiration from his forehead. 

A dazed look froze on his face. "Yes, I did it," the doctor repeated, "but anyone would have acted exactly as I did in the same circumstances," she added. "It's murder . . ." the Captain reminded her soberly, "still, you may have felt that you had a reason. What was it?" he ended abruptly. "I think that I should like to tell you the complete story from the very beginning," Dr. Ross said carefully, enunciating each syllable as precisely as if she were schooling a patient in how to overcome an unreasonable phobia. "I have apologies to make also-not that apologies mean much when one has almost killed an innocent man also, and caused so much trouble. However—would you ask Mr. and Mrs. Curtis and Mr. and Mrs. Kennedy to come in, Captain? 

I'd like them to hear also."

She spoke as casually as if it were an invitation to drop in for a cocktail rather than the climax of a three-day man hunt that had shattered the peace of mind of all Sutton Place and had boosted the circulation of the tabloids a hundred thousand copies a day. "Certainly," the Captain assented. "We may as well have everyone. I believe they're assembled downstairs, aren't they, Sergeant?"

As Sergeant Kearney passed the Captain on his way to the door to give directions to the patrolman stationed outside, his superior whispered in his ear. "This, Mike, is the dénouement I mentioned." 243

"Aw, hell," the Sergeant croaked, "all you meant was th' usual 'how-we-done-it' session." "Close enough," the Captain agreed.

While they waited, Dr. Ross sat calmly in her chair, apparently as poised as the first time the Captain had seen her. "Ruthless-cold-hard-the never-regret kind," the Captain diagnosed to himself. "The old dame's got guts," the Sergeant thought. "Jeez! Glad 

I ain't married to an old hatchet like her."

The apartment bell rang and the Sergeant rushed to admit a strangely tense group-Lynn and Marian, both deadly pale and clinging to each other for support; Mr. and Mrs. Eubank, obviously excited and curious; George Curtis, sober-faced and nervous; Blair Young, calm and fresh; Martin, worn and haggard but determined-appearing; Gerry West, looking bored but eager; and lastly, Jack House, bringing up the rear as if he knew what to expect but was in no hurry to hear the Captain say it.

Dr. Ross nodded toward chairs with an air of confidence which seemed to indicate that the maid would be along in a minute with tea and sandwiches. In the tenseness of their facial expressions, the 

Captain read the evident fact that the newcomers had not yet been told of the reason for the summons. Certainly there was no hint anywhere that they were being invited to sit down with a murderess whose audacious killing had kept them shuddering almost constantly for three days. House was the only exception. It may have been a strange game to him, but a good poker player never cares who his fellow players are or how high the stakes run.

The Captain indicated that Dr. Ross was to begin her story. "I have asked you here," Dr. Ross began in that same calm, reassuring tone, "because I have done you all a grave injustice. I hope you will believe me when I say that I did it only in desperation and that I am sorrier than I can say for all the trouble and suffering 

I have caused. 244

"You see-I- You see · · 

Then, that persuasive bedside voice failed entirely. Panic-stricken, Dr. Ross looked from face to face. 

Captain Wyckoff intervened gravely: "You have been asked here because Dr. Ross has just been arrested for the murder of Wilbur Moore!"

Martin sat upright in his chair. Marian stifled a scream. George began that nervous drumming with his fingers on the arm of the chair. Mrs. Eubank leaped to her feet, as if to run away. House yawned.

Once again, Dr. Ross was herself. "Thank you, Captain, I just could not say it. "Yes," she emphasized, as if to make things clear to a slightly foggy patient, "I did kill Wilbur Moore, but I am not sorry about that! He deserved it. I am sorry, though, that I involved all you people in an affair of which you were entirely innocent. I want you to know my story." 

She turned toward George Curtis. "I did not have the pleasure of meeting you or your lovely wife in San Francisco, Mr. Curtis. It was my bad fortune to meet Wilbur 

Moore instead. During the past three days I have thought how much you and I had in common. You were victimized by him sadly, too. You had reason to detest him, but not as intensely as I did. You see, he stole only money from you—but he destroyed my character.

He ruined my life. I thought for eight years that I could put the fragments together and, with your money, I almost succeeded." "With my money?" George repeated carefully, a piercingly cold look gathering in his eyes. "I've been scouring the country. . . ." "Yes, with your money," Dr. Ross echoed. "It was your money that transformed me from the shy, embittered, simple nurse of San 

Francisco days into the successful psychiatrist that I am today." "How?" George demanded, his fingers drumming more rapidly than ever. 245

Dr. Ross drew a long breath and continued: "Wilbur Moore and I came from the same small town in Southern California. That was before Southern California became a winter vacation spot and small towns in that section were places to escape from, decidedly. "I escaped to San Francisco and nursing, only to discover that a career in a large, strange city was not enough. "As you can see, I have never been beautiful. I used to be awkward, large and dreadfully shy-how shocked some of my patients would be to hear this confession, but I imagine they'll read all about it in the newspapers and I yearned for companionship. Moore provided it. When he moved to the rooming house where I lived, I was stupid enough to be flattered by the small attentions he paid me. At first it embarrassed me, a large woman, to be seen going about with such a small man, but neither of us had any friends in the city so it didn't matter. Besides, he was so kind and friendly, and 

I could not choose my admirers. He was the only beau that I had, or ever had had. Those San Francisco days shame me, but I still remember how happy I was-for a few months. "Gradually, he began to borrow money from me-first small and then larger and larger sums. What he did with the money, I still don't know, but it's not difficult to imagine now. Anyway the situation reached the point where my savings were gone and I virtually supported Moore for some months out of my modest earnings." 

George scowled and whistled softly through his teeth. His eyebrows puckered as if he didn't yet understand. 

Dr. Ross nodded in complete understanding and resumed: "I guess that we'll never find out about that for certain, Mr. 

Curtis. You will have to take my word for the fact that he borrowed all I had. But, to go on with my story-I think the landlady suspected our relationship, but she never mentioned it. I suppose she 246

was glad to get the room rent regularly without inquiring too much about its source. "Then, came the day of his arrest. He was prepared for it because that morning he gave me a check for a bag which he said he'd left in the parcel room at the Ferry Building. He told me to claim the bag and keep it safe and unopened until he saw me again. I brought it home and when I read in the newspapers of his arrest I opened it and found your money. "My first great misfortune was meeting Moore in San Francisco; the bag was the second. Of course, I should have returned your money at once. But I was angry with Moore. I thought of all the money he had taken from me. I knew that my nursing career would be ruined if my connection with Moore became known, and that I might even be placed under arrest as an accomplice. I succeeded in convincing myself, Mr. Curtis, that I was entitled to your money, even though it was a much larger sum than I had lost. So I kept it and went to Vienna, after first throwing Dr. Kether off the trail in case there were inquiries." "So, you never went to Chicago at all?" George inquired suddenly. "And how do you happen to know about that?" the Captain demanded. "Why. eh, eh," George stammered. "Never mind, now. We'll settle that later," the detective promised. "Continue, Dr. Ross." "Well," Dr. Ross went on, "five years in Vienna made me the celebrated psychiatrist that I am today-or was until today-with an assumed name and a well-paying practice here in New York. I suppose that landlady never told the police about my friendship with Moore because the police never tried to question me or to follow me. It would have been better all around if she had." "Yes," George agreed between frozen lips. 247

"Then," Dr. Ross resumed, ignoring George's remark, "three days ago the reckoning came, as I had known that it would-somewhere, sometime. Moore came to this apartment. He rode up in the service elevator, wearing the white coat of a delivery boy and carrying a package, which, I suppose, passed for groceries." "Why all that nonsense?" Captain Wyckoff interrupted. "Oh, he tried the front door first," Dr. Ross replied, "but I refused to see him when he was announced from downstairs." ". . . and so you got away with it . . . for a while,” the Captain commented bitterly. "I imagine the disguise was the reason no one recalled seeing him enter the building," the psychiatrist continued, without rancor. "He demanded the money and threatened to expose me, if I didn't pay at once. "I knew that paying would be only the beginning of years of blackmail, so I refused and dared him to go to the police with his story-a bit of obvious psychology which didn't work, incidentally. 

We argued for an hour. Finally, goaded to a fury by his taunts and threats, I picked up that heavy iron poker that stands over there by the fireplace and struck him on the back of the head.” 

With one accord eleven pairs of eyes turned toward the fireplace where a three-foot black iron rod stood-an ominous reminder that Dr. Ross' calm recital was a confession of brutal murder committed in such an extremely repugnant manner that probably few of those present would have dared to suspect-even to themselves—that it had been perpetrated by a woman. "You have lived three days with that weapon always before your eyes," the Captain remarked. "Hasn't it . . ." "Not lived-endured," the woman doctor pleaded.

She paused for breath and then continued with a rush of words: "I don't know whether I intended to kill him or not. Now, I suppose that I must have. Certainly, and probably instinctively, I 248

chose a most vulnerable spot. Then, I was confused and frightened.

All that is over. I left him lying here on the livingroom floor." 

She glanced at the carpet before her. Mrs. Eubank shivered and pushed her chair back toward the wall. "There wasn't any blood," the doctor continued, “and I could not be certain for a few minutes that he was dead. When I was certain, I put on my hat and coat, intending to walk around outside and to consider what I should do next. My first impulse was the river. I thought at the time that that would be the easiest way out, and perhaps I was right. Fate, however, intervened, as it so often does with all of us. "You entered the elevator at this floor, Mrs. Curtis, and that mischance almost cost your husband his life. You preceded me into the elevator and I heard you say to Charlie, the elevator man, 'Here's the key to 8B. Mrs. Kennedy won't return until later.' I watched him tuck it into that little rack on the side wall of the elevator car where he always puts keys that are entrusted to him by tenants. "Immediately a way out of all my troubles, or so it seemed at that moment, struck me and, thoughtless of the cost to all of you, I employed it. That is my greatest cause for regret now. I don't particularly regret having taken your money, Mr. Curtis, for I'm certain that you could afford it, but what I did then put a blot on the names of several persons, none of whom had ever done any harm. "Perhaps," she went on, smiling faintly for the first time, "Captain Wyckoff will do his best to repair the damage that I did so thoughtlessly three days ago. Never before had I intentionally harmed a a person in all my life."

The Captain made no comment, and slowly Dr. Ross continued her story: "Unluckily again, I had two keys to my apartment in my hand- 249

bag. The house provides two and I carry them on the same key ring. I slipped one off the ring and, after you left the elevator, Mrs.

Curtis, I placed it on the rack beside the key to 8B which you had given to Charlie. I told Charlie that I was leaving it for a friend who might call while I was out. I walked to the corner, turned back, told Charlie that I had forgotten something and took the key to 8B from the rack instead of my own. They were not marked, so 

Charlie couldn't tell the difference. "First, I unlocked the door to your apartment, Mr. Kennedy.

Then, using my second key, I entered my apartment. Quickly, I removed the identifying marks from Moore's clothing and snatched off his coat and shoes. Watching carefully, I carried the body to the 

Kennedy apartment and placed it in the clothes closet where it was found hours later. Then, again most unhappily, another impulse struck me. "I was crazy with fear, and the possibility of throwing suspicion on another person for my crime occurred to me as my only salvation. I saw the bolo on the foyer wall. I took it and left, locking the door carefully behind me. "I put Moore's shoes, coat and, the labels I had torn from his clothing into the incinerator at once. No one saw me at any time. 

I hid the bolo away in the back of a closet in my apartment here. 

Then, I went out again and left the key to 8B on the elevator rack. 

You know most of the rest," she concluded weakly. "Not entirely, Dr. Ross," the Captain said immediately. "Did you follow Mr. Curtis out last night in order to attack him down on the pier?" "No. I had observed the time when he usually walked his dog -Susie, isn't that the name?" she leaned toward George for confirmation. "A cute little tyke! I had seen them together in the elevator many times, and I had noticed from my windows, which command a good view of the riverfront, that Mr. Curtis usually went out alone with her about ten o'clock in the evening. Last night, 250

I waited down at the pier and struck you, Mr. Curtis, with the bolo which I had taken from the Kennedy apartment. "Fortunately for you, and even more so for me because now I don't have the blood of an innocent person on my conscience, a car turned into Fifty-fourth Street from First Avenue, just as I was swinging the bolo. It frightened me. I tossed the bolo into the water and jumped into that sand yard. I heard Susie barking to stop the motorist. By the time he'd left his car, I was over on Fifty-third 

Street and on my way downtown where I rode the ferryboat to Staten Island and back. I stood out on deck and the salty chill restored my poise." "Why did you feel it was necessary to kill Mr. Curtis?" the Captain asked quickly, and again George felt cold shivers racing up and down his spine. "What we scientists call 'murderer's complex,' Captain. I knew that Moore's death would cause Mr. Curtis to renew his search for his money. Then, the newspapers printed yesterday afternoon all that he had told you about Moore, but I could not be certain that everything had been printed. I felt certain that Mr. Curtis had found out about me from his detective agency. If he had not told you, my one chance was to make certain that he did not." 

Captain Wyckoff leveled an accusing finger at George. "So I was right," he said slowly. Then he turned back to

Dr. Ross. 

"One thing more, Dr. Ross. It was you who phoned Mrs. Curtis last night after the attack?” "Yes." "And to the Kennedy apartment also?" "Yes." "Why?" "I had a feeling that Mrs. Kennedy probably knew about me also. . . ." "And what?" the Captain insisted. 251

"I planned to threaten her not to talk," the psychiatrist admitted at last, "but my nerve failed me." "You need not have worried about that," the detective commented. "Mrs. Kennedy was not talking." "... in a big way," the Sergeant added. "Suppose she had talked, Dr. Ross," the Captain continued, "what would you have done?" 

The psychiatrist didn't answer. The Captain turned to Lynn: "I think the answer is apparent, Mrs. Kennedy. Your name was next on the list." 

Lynn shivered. 

Again the Captain addressed Dr. Ross: "The attack on George Curtis was your mistake. All murderers make them, if you are still interested in the subject of the psychopathic behavior of murderers. However, you'll have plenty of time to study your own mind and reactions . . . in a place where you won't be tempted to do further harm to these very foolish people. "Sergeant " · · 

As he made a gesture toward Dr. Ross, the Sergeant stepped forward. 

At that instant, Dr. Ross' imperturbable calm deserted her. For the first time, the full horror of her crime impressed itself upon her consciousness. Her face whitened. Her eyes reflected the terror in her mind. She slumped forward in her chair, her quiet assurance forever broken. She cried dismayingly.

Untouched, Sergeant Kearney lifted her from the chair and half carried her to the apartment door where he handed her over to a group of uniformed patrolmen with the terse order: "Headquarters!"

When the Sergeant had returned to his place, the Captain directed: "You're next, House. This seems to be clean-up day. Why did you dash out of the movies leaving Helen Devere there?" 252

"Does it matter now?" the confidence man responded jauntily. "It does. We have solved a murder and placed responsibility for the attack on Mr. Curtis. There remains the matter of Helen 

Devere's suicide." "I just went over to Jake's to put a couple of bets on the horses, Captain." "You could have done that by telephone." "I don't trust telephones, Captain." "You don't trust anything or anyone, House, and I certainly reciprocate the feeling as far as you're concerned." "That's the honest truth, Captain. Jake will back me up." "He already has, but I still don't believe it. I know this for certain, however: you were the cause of Helen Devere's suicide.

She killed herself because she was certain you were mixed up in the Moore killing. I can't arrest you for anything, but I believe the knowledge that you were the cause of the death of the one person—that is, the one decent person-in all this world who had any kindly feelings toward you whatsoever will be a worse punishment for you than anything the law could devise." "No, Captain," House stammered. "It's the truth, and you know it," the Captain shouted. "What's more, I believe that you helped Moore to locate Dr. Ross. Perhaps you even suggested the method he used in order to gain admittance to this apartment. Again, I can't prove it, but I know it. Helen 

Devere knew it. That explains your absence from the movies. Now, get!" 

House ran from the apartment. It was doubtful if the debonair 

Jackson Hatteras appreciated immediately the full depth of the Captain's accusation, but that, as the Captain had said, was a punishment which he would carry to the grave. ❝Items No. 1 and 2 disposed of," the Captain announced quietly as the door closed on House: "Suppose the rest of us move to your apartment, Mr. Kennedy. 253

There's something about this room that chills me." 

When they were settled in chairs in the Kennedy livingroom, the detective again took the floor: "Now, this isn't all over, as some of you people may be hoping. 

There are still some answers to be given, and I imagine that I won't have so much difficulty in getting straight ones as I have had for the past three days. "You've been involved in a mess of trouble and paraded across the headlines. Most of this has been unnecessary. You could have avoided it, but you refused. "You, Mrs. Kennedy, might have saved a life-Helen Devere's --if you had told us that first night what it took three days for us to learn-and you could have told us most of it!" 

That whitish cast in the Captain's left eye seemed to intensify the coldness of his gaze as he demanded of Lynn: "Why didn't you? Why didn't you identify Moore at once? 

Why did you conceal your knowledge about him? Why did you find it necessary to lie to us?" 

Lynn made little whimpering noises and covered her face with her hands, but didn't answer.

The Captain continued his stormy questioning: "Why did you appeal to Mr. Curtis for assistance after your arrest? Why did you mislead your husband, as well as us, concerning your whereabouts on the afternoon of Moore's murder? Why ?" 

In a thin voice which didn't carry conviction, Lynn answered, "I was protecting friends. You know that."

The Captain looked at her thoughtfully for a moment, before resuming: 

"Perhaps it isn't possible for you to stick to the literal truth. 

Actually, you were protecting your own activities, weren't you?

If you'd only realized that the police aren't interested in such things that we were trying to catch a murderer . . ." 254

Lynn jumped to her feet, her flashing eyes picturing better than words the anger that raged within her. "Martin," she screamed, “I won't have it. Make him stop insulting me. I've stood all I can."

Martin looked at her for only a second before he astonished everyone by replying: “Sit down and take your medicine! It's true. I know it. West certainly does, too, and God knows how many other people as well."

Gerry arose, his face suddenly gray. "It's true, Martin. I don't deny it. You'd never have lost her, if you'd appreciated what you had. She's bored with you." 

Martin smiled wearily: "I made up my mind several days ago to let you appreciate her. 

I'm tired."

Gerry stood there, apparently undecided and bewildered. This was not the way in which he'd planned it to happen. It was Lynn who said: "Very well. Then, let's go, Gerry. I, for one, am glad it's settled -at last." 

She held her head very high and didn't once glance at Martin as she crossed the room and stood beside Gerry. She said something in a low tone which the rest couldn't hear, but Blair thought it sounded like, "Now, we'll begin to live."

Gerry took her arm and without another word or a backward glance they left the apartment. 

The Captain said, "I'm sorry, Kennedy."

Martin sank deeper into his chair and answered with a faint smile on his face. "It's all right. It's been coming for a long time, and I'm glad it's a clean break."

Blair gave him a soft, warm look, but there was a little twinkle in her eyes as she predicted: "At any rate, Lynn will love Reno." 255

"Yeah, lady," the Sergeant contributed, "it's a long woim that has no toining."

Captain Wyckoff permitted himself a brief grin over the Sergeant's rare display of acumen, but turned stormily on George 

Curtis: 

"You, Mr. Curtis, are equally guilty of criminal neglect!"

Pointing a ruthless finger at George and glaring straight from those steel-like eyes, he continued to denounce: "You didn't play ball with us at any time. You deliberately concealed important information and misled us whenever possible. 

I have no sympathy for you and whatever bad publicity you and your firm receives is largely of your own doing. Be honest with us next time!"

George tapped nervously on the arm of his chair again, and the Captain continued without waiting for an answer where obviously there was no answer that could be made. "You knew about Hilda Cross all the time," he went on accusingly. "Not until Moore tried to find her in San Francisco after his release from Folsom," George protested. "That's exactly what I'm talking about," the detective thundered. "That almost cost you your life. Moore knew he was being tracked, and he probably told Dr. Ross. Just to keep the record straight: that was exactly what I was trying to persuade you to tell me last night. You knew it then, didn't you?" "Yes," George admitted, beating out a rapid tattoo. 

Captain Wyckoff ignored him and turned on Marian: "You, Mrs. Curtis, are to blame too. You knew something of all this. How much I don't care now, but it was apparent from the beginning that there was a conspiracy between you and Mrs. 

Kennedy to keep us as much in the dark as possible." "I did burn those reports from the Eagle Agency which told 256

all about it," Marian confessed nervously. "I'll never do anything like that again," she wound up, with a threatening look at her husband. "I fervently hope that you never have to again," the Captain replied. "Well, I guess that's all. The Kennedy murder case is closed." "Me for Canarsie and no more bean woik," the Sergeant added. 

The Curtises and the Eubanks departed, but still the Captain and his aid remained. "Thanks for what you've done, Captain," Martin said. "Do you mind straightening us out on a few points?" "That's always a pleasure after the crime's been solved, and one that no detective ever denies himself," the Captain answered affably. "Whatta yuh think we been hangin' round fer?" the Sergeant demanded much more directly. "Money don't mean nuthin' to us; we're public soivents." "How did you know that Jack House wasn't the killer?" Martin asked. "Well," the Captain answered slowly, relaxing again into that quiet, conversational tone which was so characteristic of him, “it wasn't his kind of a job in the first place. It didn't have his stamp, and criminals seldom vary their technique; that's why they all get caught eventually. I honestly believe that House saw Moore before Moore called on Dr. Ross. Probably they made connections over at Jake's place. Possibly House did supply the information about how Moore could enter the apartment. All that would have been fine blackmail material for use later on in case Moore did collect the money, and blackmail was and is Jack House's line. "Of course, all along Moore depended upon Dr. Ross. It must have been quite a shock when she refused to see him. In California, she was just the type on which he depended. He chose his type 257

well, but she changed while he was in Folsom. She turned out to be a vastly different person from the Hilda Cross he had known in the West." "Very reasonable, Captain," Blair agreed, "but I could have figured that much myself. How did you really hit on Dr. Ross as the murderer?" "Well, I didn't know for certain until just before we made the arrest, but, still, the Sergeant and I ought to take credit for having narrowed things down considerably long before then." "And just how did you do that?" Blair asked. "Do you have to be coaxed?" 

Captain Wyckoff grinned. "No, Miss Young, we'll be glad to tell all. It went something like this: in the first place, we didn't believe that Moore had been killed in this apartment.' 

"Could you tell that from the condition and position of the body?" Martin inquired. "Oh, that rigor mortis business. That's drivel, Mr. Kennedy.

No, just that there should have been some evidence of Moore's having been here prior to his death. We couldn't find anything . . . not even a foot track or a fingerprint, much less more tangible evidence. "Well, that was the first problem. If Moore wasn't murdered here, where was he killed? The obvious—and only-answer was somewhere nearby, which meant somewhere on this floor because in an apartment house of this size it wouldn't be possible to carry a dead man around very much without being seen by someone. 

In my mind that narrowed the search down to the other three apartments on this floor. "Then, when Mrs. Kennedy-perhaps I should say the 'then'

Mrs. Kennedy," he amended with a smile, "told us of leaving the key with Charlie when she went out, it was easier still. Anyone living on this floor would have been in a good spot to watch Mrs. 258

Kennedy's movements, and possibly to overhear her telling Charlie that she was leaving the key. Both Dr. Ross and Mrs. Eubank admitted being home on the afternoon of the murder. Either one could have been a good suspect, and, of course, House at that time, which was before we learned about his usual type of criminal activities. "Unfortunately, there wasn't anything that connected the Eubanks with either you, Mr. Kennedy, or the Curtises, whose stake was apparent from the beginning. It was also unfortunate that Dr. 

Ross had covered up so completely on her past life. We never could get a checkup on her, and we didn't learn about her California background until the very last. "You, Mr. Kennedy, seemed to have a solid alibi for the afternoon of the murder, and you, Miss Young, didn't appear to have any motive, so I might say right here that both of you were definitely not suspects right from the start. "Then, came the attack on George Curtis. That eliminated him and probably his wife also. Without a question, it exonerated Mrs. 

Kennedy because of her very apparent efforts all along to protect the Curtises. She was on their side right from the beginning, although Mike, here, did insist upon arresting her." "That was really what you call 'protective custody' then?" Blair asked. "Exactly, and we learned today that it wasn't such a bad idea after all. But to go on, that left Dr. Ross and Mr. and Mrs. Eubank, with no connection apparent between any of them and the murdered man. "Three factors then decided the issue. No. I was the method of attack on both Moore and George Curtis. Both were struck in the most vulnerable spot of all for a quick and bloodless death. 

Almost anywhere else would have been messy and slower. That suggested someone with a knowledge of the rudiments of anatomy. 

We've found that few laymen would ever think to strike an enemy 259

at the base of the skull. Some might do it by accident once, but few would make two similar attacks with similar weapons. It didn't look accidental, and it suggested a doctor. "Factor No. 2 was our discovery that Dr. Ross' apartment commanded a fine view of the riverfront. The Eubanks' didn't. There, living alone, Dr. Ross probably spent a lot of time watching the waterfront, and it would have been easy for her to note, as she did, that George Curtis—a methodical fellow-made those lonely dog-walking trips of his at about the same hour every night. "Factor No. 3 was a psychological one, and I'm proud to say that we beat Dr. Ross at her own game. Right from our first interview, she stalled me. She was always hoping that later on she might recall something of value to us. She promised to phone me, but never did. At first, I thought nothing of it, but, when it occurred several times, I became suspicious. You see, Dr. Ross was almost masculine in her directness, and she stepped right out of character when she inched away from talking directly about the murder. It was a small point, but a significant one. "Well, that was about enough for us, but what could we prove?

Nothing! That is, until Jack House and Gerry West both insisted (how West knew, I don't know nor care now) that we find out more from the Eagle Agency. When we learned that Moore had been on the trail of a nurse, the transformation into a woman psychiatrist was explainable, and it was merely a matter of making the arrest. "We never did know the details of how the exchange of keys was effected by Dr. Ross until she told us a little while ago, but that wasn't important. It was clear that she had handled it in some way, but it wasn't worth while trying to track down after it was apparent that she was our murderess."

The Captain gestured slowly and smiled to indicate that now the Kennedy murder case was not only closed, but explained. When he arose to leave the apartment, limping slowly, there was such a 260

self-satisfied air about him that his good leg seemed to strut, and he hummed vaguely, "California, Here I Come."

Detective Sergeant Kearney strolled along in his wake, whistling softly. 

It was sometime after they left that Blair asked suddenly: "What was the Sergeant whistling, Martin?" "It sounded like a familiar bit from Mendelssohn. I marched to it once, but "But what?" .. · Hers was a voice that was warm with sympathy. "Blair, stop feeling sorry for me," Martin ordered. "Don't you realize that I'm happy that it's all over. I know now that all women aren't like that, but my hopes went overboard the other night when you said you didn't go for reporters, but could · · · get all droopy over a nice fat importer."

He sat down beside her on the sofa and, while he looked tired and worn-out, there was a new jauntiness about him. "That's true. I did say that." "Well, I'm not fat and I'm not an importer." 

Blair thought for a moment and said gaily: "Well, we could feed you up some-and it doesn't matter so much about your not being an importer. That was really just a whim." 

THE END
